B ​ C BC Toda chain I: reflection operator and eigenfunctions


Abstract
We obtain Gauss–Givental integral representation for the eigenfunctions of quantum Toda chain with boundary interaction of B ​ C BC type. For this we introduce reflection operator satisfying reflection equation with DST chain Lax matrices. Besides, we define Baxter operators for B ​ C BC Toda chain, prove their commutativity with Hamiltonians and derive the corresponding Baxter equation.



N. Belousov
†
, S. Derkachov
⋄†
, S. Khoroshkin

Contents

1
Introduction

In the present paper we study quantum Toda chain with one-sided boundary interaction of
BC
type. This is a system of
n
particles with coordinates
x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, governed by Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{BC}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}e^{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}+2\alpha\,e^{-x_{1}}+\beta^{2}e^{-2x_{1}},

(1.1)

where
\alpha,\beta
are parameters. As shown in
[
Skl2
,
I
]
, this model is integrable: the above Hamiltonian belongs to a
commuting
family of differential operators
\mathbb{H}_{s}
of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{s}=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\ldots<j_{s}\leq n}\partial_{x_{j_{1}}}^{2}\cdots\partial_{x_{j_{s}}}^{2}+\text{ lower order terms }\qquad(s=1,\ldots,n),

(1.2)

that is
\mathbb{H}_{1}=-\mathbb{H}_{BC}
, see Section
1.2.1
for details.

For
\alpha\beta=0
this model is related to classical Lie algebras of
GL_{n}
(
\alpha=\beta=0
),
B_{n}
(
\alpha\not=0
,
\beta=0
) and
C_{n}
(
\alpha=0
,
\beta\not=0
) types
[
Kost
]
. In these special cases the joint eigenfunctions of the Hamiltonians can be constructed using representation theory of corresponding Lie group. In this paper we develop an alternative approach based on certain solutions of Yang–Baxter and reflection equations, which also covers the general case
\alpha\beta\not=0
.

In what follows we impose restrictions on the boundary parameters

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}>0,\qquad\beta>0.

(1.3)

The reason is that already in the simplest case of one particle these conditions ensure that the Hamiltonian (
1.1
) has no discrete spectra
1
1

1

In fact, the same is true if
\alpha/\beta=-1/2
, but we exclude this case to simplify matters.
, see
[
DL
, Theorem 4.3]
. The case of
B
Toda chain is accessed by taking the limit
\beta\to 0
.

Remark 1
.

If one restores the Planck constant
\hbar
in the Hamiltonian, the first condition (
1.3
) becomes
\alpha>-\hbar\beta/2
.

The spectral problem for one particle

\displaystyle\bigl(-\partial_{x}^{2}+2\alpha\,e^{-x}+\beta^{2}e^{-2x}\bigr)\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda^{2}\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{R},

(1.4)

has a unique solution decaying as
x\to-\infty
, which is given by Whittaker function

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{e^{\frac{x}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\beta}}\,W_{-\frac{\alpha}{\beta},-\mathrm{i}\lambda}(2\beta e^{-x}),

(1.5)

see
[
DLMF
,
Chapter 13
]
. The latter admits the following integral representation
[
DLMF
,
(13.16.5)
]

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{e^{\frac{x}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\beta}}\,\frac{2^{2\mathrm{i}\lambda}\,(2\beta e^{-x})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}-\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigr)}\int_{1}^{\infty}dt\;e^{-\beta e^{-x}t}\,(t+1)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\,(t-1)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}.

(1.6)

Equivalently, denoting

\displaystyle g=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}>0

(1.7)

and changing integration variable
t=e^{z}/\beta
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dz\;e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda(x-2z)-e^{z-x}}\,(1+\beta e^{-z})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-z})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+g-1}.

(1.8)

The main result of the paper is the generalization of the integral representation (
1.8
) to the case of
n
particles. Denote tuples of
n
variables and sums of their components

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{n}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),\qquad\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}=x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}.

(1.9)

Besides, by
\theta(x)
denote the Heaviside step function

\displaystyle\theta(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&&x\geq 0,\\[4.0pt]
&0,&&x<0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.10)

Theorem 1
.

The joint eigenfunctions
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
of the commuting Hamiltonians (
1.2
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{s}\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=(-1)^{s}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\ldots<j_{s}\leq n}\lambda_{j_{1}}^{2}\cdots\lambda_{j_{s}}^{2}\;\;\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})

(1.11)

admit the recursive integral representation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})&=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n})}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{y}_{n-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{z}_{n}\;\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}+\underline{\bm{y}}_{n-1}-2\underline{\bm{z}}_{n}\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
&-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{z_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-z_{j+1}}+e^{y_{j}-z_{j+1}})-e^{z_{n}-x_{n}}\biggr)\\[6.0pt]
&\times(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}+g-1}\;\theta(z_{1}-\ln\beta)\;\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}(\bm{y}_{n-1}),\end{aligned}

(1.12)

with the recursion starting from the one-particle formula (
1.8
).

By tradition, this is called
Gauss–Givental representation
. Changing the integration variable
z_{1}=\ln(\beta\,\ch z_{1}^{\prime})
we obtain representation conjectured in
[
GLO3
, Conjecture 1.2]
. As argued there, in the special cases
\beta\to 0
or
\alpha=0
the latter coincides with the formulas for
B
or
C
type models, which were obtained in
[
GLO2
]
using representation theory of Lie groups. In particular, in the limit
\beta\to 0
the last line of (
1.12
) simplifies

\displaystyle(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}+g-1}\,\theta(z_{1}-\ln\beta)\to e^{-2\alpha\,e^{-z_{1}}}.

(1.13)

Example 1
.

In the case of two particles

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Psi_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1})\,\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2})}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dz_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dz_{2}\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dw\\[6.0pt]
&\quad\times e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}+y-2z_{1}-2z_{2})+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}(y-2w)-e^{z_{1}-x_{1}}-e^{x_{1}-z_{2}}-e^{z_{2}-x_{2}}-e^{z_{1}-y}-e^{y-z_{2}}-e^{w-y}}\\[6.0pt]
&\quad\times(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}+g-1}\,(1+\beta e^{-w})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-w})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}+g-1}.\end{aligned}

(1.14)

In the rest of Section
1
we introduce the necessary objects and outline the proof of the above theorem. In addition to the eigenfunctions, we study the so-called
Baxter operators
. As we show in the subsequent paper
[
BDK
]
, these operators play an important role in deriving the “dual” Mellin–Barnes integral representation.

Most of the ideas we use for the construction of the
BC
Toda eigenfunctions are more easily presented in the case of the
GL
Toda chain (
\alpha=\beta=0
). Moreover, many of the objects we introduce appear in both models. Therefore, below we first consider the
GL
Toda chain.

1.1
GL
Toda chain

1.1.1
Commuting Hamiltonians

The Hamiltonian defining the
GL
Toda chain is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{GL}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}e^{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}.

(1.15)

Recall the construction of higher order Hamiltonians commuting with it
[
F
,
Gaud
,
Skl1
]
.
Introduce Toda Lax matrix associated with
j
-th particle

\displaystyle L_{j}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{j}}&e^{-x_{j}}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{j}}&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.16)

and Yang’s
R
-matrix

R(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}&0&0&0\\
0&u&\mathrm{i}&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}&u&0\\
0&0&0&u+\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.17)

Together they satisfy the Yang–Baxter equation

\displaystyle R(u-v)\,\bigl(L_{j}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes L_{j}(v)\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes L_{j}(v)\bigr)\,\bigl(L_{j}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u-v),

(1.18)

where
\bm{1}
is
2\times 2
identity matrix, the check is straightforward. Next define the monodromy matrix

\displaystyle T_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\cdots L_{1}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}A_{n}(u)&B_{n}(u)\\[4.0pt]
C_{n}(u)&D_{n}(u)\end{pmatrix},

(1.19)

whose elements are differential operators acting on all coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. By standard arguments
[
Sl
, Proposition 3.1]
, from the equation (
1.18
) we obtain

\displaystyle R(u-v)\,\bigl(T_{n}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes T_{n}(v)\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes T_{n}(v)\bigr)\,\bigl(T_{n}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u-v).

(1.20)

The equality of
(1,1)
elements in this matrix relation implies commutativity

\displaystyle\bigl[A_{n}(u),A_{n}(v)\bigr]=0.

(1.21)

As a result, coefficients of the operator
A_{n}(u)
commute with each other

\displaystyle A_{n}(u)=u^{n}+\sum_{s=1}^{n}u^{n-s}\mathrm{H}_{s},\qquad[\mathrm{H}_{s},\mathrm{H}_{r}]=0.

(1.22)

The first two are

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{j}},\qquad\mathrm{H}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathrm{H}_{1}^{2}-\mathrm{H}_{GL}\bigr).

(1.23)

Hence, the operators
\mathrm{H}_{s}
also commute with the quadratic Hamiltonian (
1.15
).

1.1.2
Eigenfunctions

Now let us construct the joint eigenfunctions of the Hamiltonians

\displaystyle A_{n}(u)\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(u-\lambda_{j})\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})

(1.24)

parametrized by zeroes of the
A_{n}(u)
eigenvalue. For this introduce DST (dimer self-trapping) chain Lax matrix
[
KSS
]

\displaystyle M_{a}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{a}}&e^{-x_{a}}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{a}}\partial_{x_{a}}&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix},

(1.25)

which satisfies the same Yang–Baxter equation (
1.18
), as Toda Lax matrix,

\displaystyle R(u-v)\,\bigl(M_{a}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes M_{a}(v)\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes M_{a}(v)\bigr)\,\bigl(M_{a}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u-v).

(1.26)

The key object in the construction of eigenfunctions is the
\mathcal{R}
-operator intertwining Toda and DST Lax matrices

\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,M_{2}(u-v)=M_{2}(u-v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v).

(1.27)

It is an integral operator acting on functions of two variables
\varphi(x_{1},x_{2})
by the formula

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}\bigr)\,\varphi(y,x_{1})

(1.28)

derived in
[
Skl3
, Section 3.4]
. The matrix relation (
1.27
) contains four differential equations for the kernel of this operator, which can be verified directly. In Section
7.3
we describe the space of functions on which this operator is well defined and for which the relation (
1.27
) holds.

Analogously to the monodromy matrix (
1.19
), we define monodromy operator

\displaystyle U_{na}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)

(1.29)

that acts on functions of
n+1
variables
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{a})
. Again, by standard arguments, from (
1.27
) it intertwines monodromy matrix and DST Lax matrix

\displaystyle U_{na}(v)\,T_{n}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)=M_{a}(u-v)\,T_{n}(u)\,U_{na}(v),

(1.30)

for the detailed proof see Proposition
5
in Section
7.4
.

To construct the eigenfunctions of
A_{n}(u)
we replace
n\to n-1
,
a\to n
in the last relation and consider the equality of
(1,1)
matrix elements

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &U_{n-1,n}(v)\bigl(A_{n-1}(u)\,(u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}})-B_{n-1}(u)\,e^{x_{n}}\partial_{x_{n}}\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
&\quad=\bigl((u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}})\,A_{n-1}(u)+e^{-x_{n}}\,C_{n-1}(u)\bigr)\,U_{n-1,n}(v).\end{aligned}

(1.31)

If we act on a function of
n-1
variables
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})
, the first line simplifies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(u-v)\,U_{n-1,n}(v)\,A_{n-1}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})\\[6.0pt]
&\quad=\bigl((u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}})\,A_{n-1}(u)+e^{-x_{n}}\,C_{n-1}(u)\bigr)\,U_{n-1,n}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1}).\end{aligned}

(1.32)

Besides, inductive formula (
1.19
) implies the equality

\bigl((u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}})\,A_{n-1}(u)+e^{-x_{n}}\,C_{n-1}(u)\bigr)e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\\[6.0pt]
=e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\bigl((u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}})\,A_{n-1}(u)+e^{-x_{n}}\,C_{n-1}(u)\bigr)=e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,A_{n}(u),

(1.33)

which we can use to simplify the second line of (
1.31
).

Define the
raising operator

\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)
acting on functions of
n-1
variables

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n}}\,U_{n-1,n}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})}=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n}}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})}.

(1.34)

Due to (
1.32
) and (
1.33
) it intertwines Hamiltonians with
n
and
n-1
variables

\displaystyle A_{n}(u)\,\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)=(u-\lambda)\,\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,A_{n-1}(u),

(1.35)

where for
n=1
we denote
A_{0}(u)=1
. As a consequence, the eigenfunctions satisfying (
1.24
) can be constructed in a recursive way

\displaystyle\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\Lambda_{n}(\lambda_{n})\,\Lambda_{n-1}(\lambda_{n-1})\cdots\Lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1.

(1.36)

From the explicit formula for the
\mathcal{R}
-operator (
1.28
) and definition (
1.34
) we have

\displaystyle\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{y}_{n-1}\,\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}-\underline{\bm{y}}_{n-1}\bigr)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{x_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{y_{j}-x_{j+1}})\biggr)\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n-1}).

(1.37)

This gives the well-known Gauss–Givental representation of
GL
Toda eigenfunctions, obtained by different methods in the works
[
Giv
,
GKLO
,
Sil
]
. The above derivation appears to be new, but it is very similar to the known construction for the spin chain
[
ADV1
]
.

Example 2
.

In the case of one and two particles

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda_{1}}(x_{1})=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}x_{1}},\qquad\Phi_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}y}.

(1.38)

It can be shown that raising operators satisfy the exchange relation

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\Lambda_{n-1}(\rho)=\Lambda_{n}(\rho)\,\Lambda_{n-1}(\lambda),

(1.39)

so that the eigenfunctions are symmetric with respect to spectral parameters

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\Phi_{\lambda_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\lambda_{\sigma(n)}}(\bm{x}_{n}),\qquad\sigma\in S_{n},

(1.40)

see
[
Sil
, Theorem 1]
. Thus, the spectra of Hamiltonians (
1.24
) is nondegenerate.

Let us mention without proof that from the identity (
1.30
) one can also derive the relations

\displaystyle B_{n}(\lambda)\,\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}^{n-1}\,\Lambda_{n}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}),

(1.41)

\displaystyle C_{n}(\lambda)\,\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}^{-n-1}\,\Lambda_{n}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}).

(1.42)

Using them together with symmetry in spectral parameters (
1.40
) one deduces the action of non-diagonal elements of monodromy matrix on eigenfunctions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &B(\lambda_{j})\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\mathrm{i}^{n-1}\,\Phi_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{j}+\mathrm{i},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}),\\[6.0pt]
&C(\lambda_{j})\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\mathrm{i}^{-n-1}\,\Phi_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{j}-\mathrm{i},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}).\end{aligned}

(1.43)

The last formula coincides with the one obtained in
[
KL
, (3.1b)]
. These formulas show how the shift operators
e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}}
(acting in the space of spectral parameters) are represented in coordinate space. In Section
1.2.6
we give analogous statements for
BC
Toda chain.

1.1.3
Baxter operator

There is one more useful reduction of monodromy operator, which is known as
Baxter operator

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\lambda)=\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}U_{n,n+1}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})}=\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{n,n+1}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1,n+1}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})}.

(1.44)

It acts on functions of
n
coordinates
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})
by the explicit formula

\displaystyle\bigl[Q_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\,\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}-\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}\bigr)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{x_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{y_{j}-x_{j+1}})-e^{x_{n}-y_{n}}\biggr)\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n}).

(1.45)

Using the identity (
1.30
) one can deduce that it commutes with Hamiltonians

\displaystyle A_{n}(u)\,Q_{n}(\lambda)=Q_{n}(\lambda)\,A_{n}(u).

(1.46)

Furthermore, as shown in
[
GLO1
, Theorem 2.3]
, it satisfies the relations

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\lambda)\,Q_{n}(\rho)=Q_{n}(\rho)\,Q_{n}(\lambda),

(1.47)

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\lambda)\,\Lambda_{n}(\rho)=\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\lambda-\mathrm{i}\rho)\,\Lambda_{n}(\rho)\,Q_{n-1}(\lambda),

(1.48)

\displaystyle A_{n}(\lambda)\,Q_{n}(\lambda)=(-\mathrm{i})^{n}\,Q_{n}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}),

(1.49)

where the last one is called
Baxter equation
. The second identity and recursive construction of eigenfunctions (
1.36
) imply that Baxter operators are diagonalized by Hamiltonians’ eigenfunctions

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\lambda)\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\lambda-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j})\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}),

(1.50)

as it is expected from commutativity (
1.46
).

The Baxter operator is a useful tool for studying properties of eigenfunctions, for example it allows to identify Gauss–Givental representation (
1.36
) with the so-called Mellin–Barnes representation
[
GLO1
, Section 3]
. For general background on Baxter operators see
[
KS
,
Skl3
]
.

At last, let us explain the heuristic idea behind the definition (
1.44
), which can be rewritten in terms of raising operator (
1.34
)

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\lambda)=\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}U_{n,n+1}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})}=\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}e^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n+1}}\,\Lambda_{n+1}(\lambda).

(1.51)

By definition (
1.19
), Hamiltonians’ generating function satisfies the recurrence

\displaystyle A_{n+1}(u)=(u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})\,A_{n}(u)+e^{-x_{n+1}}\,C_{n}(u).

(1.52)

Hence, in the limit
x_{n+1}\to\infty
we have

\displaystyle A_{n+1}(u)\simeq(u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})\,A_{n}(u).

(1.53)

The right hand side is diagonalized by the function
e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n+1}x_{n+1}}\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
, while the left hand side is diagonalized by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n+1}}(\bm{x}_{n+1})=\Lambda_{n+1}(\lambda_{n+1})\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n+1}x_{n+1}}\,\bigl[e^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n+1}x_{n+1}}\,\Lambda_{n+1}(\lambda_{n+1})\bigr]\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}),

(1.54)

and the eigenvalues corresponding to both sides coincide. Hence, we can expect that in the limit
x_{n+1}\to\infty
these eigenfunctions coincide up to normalization
c(\bm{\lambda}_{n+1})
, which can appear from the action of operator in square brackets

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}\bigl[e^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n+1}x_{n+1}}\,\Lambda_{n+1}(\lambda_{n+1})\bigr]\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=c(\bm{\lambda}_{n+1})\,\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})

(1.55)

According to definition (
1.51
), the last equality is exactly the Baxter operator diagonalization property (
1.50
).

1.2
BC
Toda chain

1.2.1
Commuting Hamiltonians

Recall the construction of Hamiltonians for
BC
Toda chain (
1.2
), as proposed in
[
Skl2
]
, see also
[
IS
, Section 4]
. Define the boundary
K
-matrix

\displaystyle K(u)=\begin{pmatrix}-\alpha&u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\\[6.0pt]
-\beta^{2}\bigl(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\bigr)&-\alpha\end{pmatrix}.

(1.56)

Together with Yang’s
R
-matrix (
1.17
) it satisfies the reflection equation

R(u-v)\,\bigl(K(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u+v-\mathrm{i})\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes K(v)\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes K(v)\bigr)\,R(u+v-\mathrm{i})\,\bigl(K(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u-v).

(1.57)

Next introduce monodromy matrix for
BC
model

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{T}_{n}(u)&=L_{n}(u)\cdots L_{1}(u)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}\,L^{t}_{1}(-u)\cdots L^{t}_{n}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}\\[6.0pt]
&=T_{n}(u)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}\,T_{n}^{t}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{A}_{n}(u)&\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\\[4.0pt]
\mathbb{C}_{n}(u)&\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\end{pmatrix},\end{aligned}

(1.58)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{2}^{2}=\bm{1}.

(1.59)

Notice that the matrices
\sigma_{2}\,L_{j}^{t}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}
satisfy the same Yang–Baxter relation (
1.18
), as
L_{j}(u)
. This fact together with (
1.57
) imply that the monodromy matrix also satisfies reflection equation

R(u-v)\,\bigl(\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u+v-\mathrm{i})\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{n}(v)\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{n}(v)\bigr)\,R(u+v-\mathrm{i})\,\bigl(\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,R(u-v),

(1.60)

see
[
Skl2
, Proposition 2]
. The latter is equivalent to the commutation relations between elements of the monodromy matrix. In particular, equality between
(1,4)
elements gives commutativity

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathbb{B}_{n}(u),\mathbb{B}_{n}(v)\bigr]=0.

(1.61)

Consequently, the coefficients of
\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)
commute with each other

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)=(-1)^{n}\biggl(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\biggr)\biggl(u^{2n}+\sum_{s=1}^{n}u^{2(n-s)}\,\mathbb{H}_{s}\biggr),\qquad[\mathbb{H}_{s},\mathbb{H}_{r}]=0.

(1.62)

It is straightforward to check that
\mathbb{H}_{1}=-\mathbb{H}_{BC}
and
\mathbb{H}_{s}
have the form (
1.2
), so these are Hamiltonians for
BC
Toda chain.

Remark 2
.

The fact that
B(u)/(u-\mathrm{i}/2)
is an even polynomial in
u
follows from commutation relations on the entries of monodromy matrix
T_{n}(u)
, e.g. see
[
ADV2
, p. 14–15]
.

1.2.2
Eigenfunctions

Now we describe the main steps in the construction of the joint eigenfunctions

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=(-1)^{n}\biggl(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\biggr)\prod_{j=1}^{n}(u^{2}-\lambda_{j}^{2})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})

(1.63)

parametrized by nontrivial zeroes of
\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)
eigenvalue. As in the case of the
GL
Toda chain, this requires working with integral operators. Let us postpone the discussion of the function spaces on which these operators are well defined to Section
1.2.4
.

In a nutshell, to construct the eigenfunctions we only need two objects: the
\mathcal{R}
-operator (
1.28
) and the
reflection operator

\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)
. The latter acts on functions of one variable
\varphi(x_{a})
and satisfies equation with DST Lax matrices (
1.25
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}(u-v)\sigma_{2}=M_{a}(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathcal{K}_{a}(v).

(1.64)

In Section
2
we show that it is given by the formula

\bigl[\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{a})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}v}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}v)}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\,\exp\bigl(-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x_{a}}\bigr)\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\\
\times\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1}\,\varphi(-y),

(1.65)

where as before
g=1/2+\alpha/\beta
. Note that in terms of this operator the one-particle eigenfunction formula (
1.8
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda_{1}}(x_{1})=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}x_{1}}\,\mathcal{K}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1.

(1.66)

Let us also introduce the
reflected

\mathcal{R}
-operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)=r_{1}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,r_{1},\qquad r_{1}\colon\;\varphi(x_{1})\mapsto\varphi(-x_{1}),

(1.67)

which intertwines Toda Lax matrix and
transposed
DST matrix

\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,M^{t}_{2}(-u-v)\,L_{1}(u)=L_{1}(u)\,M^{t}_{2}(-u-v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v),

(1.68)

as a straightforward consequence of the relation (
1.27
).
Explicitly, it is given by

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(y-x_{1})-e^{y-x_{1}}-e^{-y-x_{2}}\bigr)\,\varphi(y,-x_{1}).

(1.69)

Next, analogously to the monodromy matrix (
1.58
), define the monodromy operator for
BC
model

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)\,\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1a}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v).

(1.70)

Following the approach of Sklyanin
[
Skl2
]
, in Section
7.4
we show that the “local” relations (
1.27
), (
1.64
) and (
1.68
) imply the “global” relation for the monodromy operator

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}(u-v)\sigma_{2}\\[6.0pt]
&\quad\quad=M_{a}(u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathbb{U}_{na}(v),\end{aligned}

(1.71)

see Proposition
6
. Let us stress that this is one of the key formulas, which we use several times to obtain relations with elements of the monodromy matrix and different integral operators.

Finally, define the raising operator
\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)
that acts on functions of
n-1
coordinates

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)&=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n}}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})}\\[6.0pt]
&=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n}}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(\lambda)\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})}.\end{aligned}

(1.72)

Explicitly, it is given by the integral operator

\displaystyle\bigl[\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{y}_{n-1}\;\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{\lambda}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n-1})\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n-1})

(1.73)

with the kernel

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{\lambda}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n-1})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&d\bm{z}_{n}\;\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}+\underline{\bm{y}}_{n-1}-2\underline{\bm{z}}_{n}\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
&-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{z_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-z_{j+1}}+e^{y_{j}-z_{j+1}})-e^{z_{n}-x_{n}}\biggr)\\[6.0pt]
&\times(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+g-1}\;\theta(z_{1}-\ln\beta).\end{aligned}

(1.74)

In Section
3
we deduce from the identity (
1.71
) that it intertwines Hamiltonians with
n
and
n-1
variables

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{2}-u^{2})\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{B}_{n-1}(u).

(1.75)

Note that by definition (
1.58
) we have
\mathbb{B}_{0}=(u-\mathrm{i}/2)
. Hence, the eigenfunctions satisfying (
1.63
) are constructed in a recursive way

\displaystyle\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda_{n})\cdots\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda_{n})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}(\bm{x}_{n-1}),

(1.76)

which is equivalent to the announced formula (
1.12
).

Example 3
.

In the simplest case
\mathbb{U}_{0a}(v)=\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)
and

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda_{1}}(x_{1})=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1}x_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1.

(1.77)

Besides, in Section
6
from the relation (
1.71
) we derive the equality

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{n}(\lambda)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)=-\beta(g+\mathrm{i}\lambda)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}).

(1.78)

This is similar to the formula (
1.42
) for
GL
model.

Remark 3
.

The matrices
M_{a}^{t}(-u)
and
\sigma_{2}M_{a}^{t}(-u)\sigma_{2}
satisfy the same Yang–Baxter equation (
1.26
), as
M_{a}(u)
. Consequently, the products

\displaystyle M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}(u-v)\sigma_{2},\qquad M_{a}(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}

(1.79)

satisfy the same reflection equation, as monodromy matrix (
1.60
). In other words, coefficients of these matrices give two representations of the same algebra, defined by reflection equation. Hence, by the formula (
1.64
) the reflection operator
\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)
intertwines these representations.

1.2.3
Baxter operator

As in the case of
GL
model, monodromy operator has one more useful reduction. Define Baxter operator for
BC
Toda chain

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)=\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}\mathbb{U}_{n,n+1}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})}=\lim_{x_{n+1}\to\infty}e^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n+1}}\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n+1}(\lambda).

(1.80)

Explicitly, it is given by the integral operator

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;\mathbb{Q}_{\lambda}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n})\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n})

(1.81)

with the kernel

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{Q}_{\lambda}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}&d\bm{z}_{n+1}\;\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}+\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}-2\underline{\bm{z}}_{n+1}\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
&-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(e^{z_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-z_{j+1}}+e^{y_{j}-z_{j+1}})\biggr)\\[6.0pt]
&\times\bigl(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+g-1}\;\theta(z_{1}-\ln\beta),\end{aligned}

(1.82)

which is convergent under assumption
\Im\lambda\in(-g,0)
. The lower bound of this interval is needed in the integral over
z_{1}
, while the upper bound — in the integral over
z_{n+1}
. The explicit expression for the kernel follows from the formula (
1.74
). The interchange of limit and integration can be justified for a suitable space of functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})
, see Corollary
5
in Section
7.5
.

The heuristic idea behind the definition (
1.80
) is the same, as for
GL
model. From (
1.58
) the Hamiltonians’ generating function satisfies the recurrence

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{B}_{n+1}(u)&=\bigl((u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)+e^{-x_{n+1}}\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\bigr)(-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})\\[6.0pt]
&-\bigl((u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})\mathbb{A}_{n}(u)+e^{-x_{n+1}}\mathbb{C}_{n}(u)\bigr)e^{-x_{n+1}},\end{aligned}

(1.83)

which in the limit
x_{n+1}\to\infty
reduces to

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n+1}(u)\simeq(u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})(-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n+1}})\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u).

(1.84)

As for
GL
model, comparison between eigenfunctions of both sides suggests that the Baxter operator (
1.80
) is diagonalized by
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
.

In Section
4
we provide further evidence for this: using the relation (
1.71
) we prove that Baxter operator commutes with Hamiltonians

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)=\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda).

(1.85)

Moreover, using the relation close to (
1.71
) in Section
5
we derive Baxter equation

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(\lambda)=-\frac{\beta(g+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}{2\lambda}\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}),

(1.86)

which implies the difference equation on the eigenvalue of Baxter operator.

In Appendix
A
we briefly explain how to prove that Baxter operators form a
commuting
family. The argument is based on the Yang–Baxter and reflection equations with operators (rather than matrices). In the subsequent work
[
BDK
]
we give a fully detailed proof of the commutativity using a different approach.

1.2.4
Bounds and function spaces

It is known that the
GL
Toda eigenfunctions
\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
with real spectral parameters decay rapidly in the classically forbidden regions
x_{j+1}\ll x_{j}
, see
[
BC
, Proposition 4.1.3]
. In Section
7.2
we prove that the
BC
Toda eigenfunctions
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
defined by the integral (
1.76
) are smooth in
\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and admit the similar bound (for
\bm{\lambda}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigl|\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|&\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j})|}\,P(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|)\\[6.0pt]
&\times\exp\Biggl(-\beta e^{-x_{1}}\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}e^{\frac{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}{2}}\,\theta(x_{j}-x_{j+1})\Biggr),\end{aligned}

(1.87)

where
P
is some polynomial. This is analogous to the known asymptotics of Whittaker function (
1.5
) in one-particle case.

Besides, in Sections
7.3
–
7.5
we investigate spaces of functions on which all operators discussed previously are well defined and relations with them hold true.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the space of smooth functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
exponentially bounded with all their derivatives, i.e. for every
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
there exist constants
a,b\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|\leq a\,e^{b(|x_{1}|+\ldots+|x_{n}|)}.

(1.88)

Also denote by
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the subspace of
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with functions having at most polynomial growth as
x_{n}\to\infty
(with all their derivatives), i.e. for every
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
there exist constants
a,b\geq 0
and polynomial
P(x_{n})
satisfying

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|\leq a\,e^{b(|x_{1}|+\ldots+|x_{n-1}|)}\times\left\{\begin{aligned} &\,P(x_{n}),&&\;x_{n}\geq 0,\\[6.0pt]
&\,e^{b|x_{n}|},&&\;x_{n}<0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.89)

Clearly, the matrix entries of Lax and monodromy matrices act invariantly on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. It is also easy to see that the operator
\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)
acts invariantly on
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see Corollary
6
). In Sections
7.3
–
7.5
we establish the analogous properties for the integral operators. The following theorem follows from combination of Proposition
2
, Corollary
5
and Remark
7
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{C}
,
\Im v>-g
and
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
. Then the operators
\mathcal{R}_{jk}(u)
,
\mathcal{K}_{j}(v)
,
\mathbb{U}_{n,n+1}(v)
,
\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(v)
act invariantly on the space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. Furthermore, for
\Im v\in(-g,0)
the operator
\mathbb{Q}_{n}(v)
maps
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

With some additional arguments this justifies all manipulations with operators, see Propositions
3
–
6
and the concluding remarks in Sections
3
–
6
. Combined together they imply the following statement.

Corollary 1
.

All relations with operators from Sections
1.2.2
and
1.2.3
hold on the spaces
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
respectively (assuming suitable restrictions on the parameters, as in Theorem
2
).

Remark 4
.

The space of exponentially tempered functions
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is natural when considering the action of the monodromy matrix entries (which contain
e^{\pm x_{j}}
). It is larger than the Whittaker Schwartz space considered by Wallach
[
W
]
, which is suited to the action of the Toda Hamiltonians rather than the monodromy matrix.

1.2.5
Relation to XXX spin chain

All of the above statements parallel those obtained recently for the open spin chain
[
ADV2
]
. This can be explained by the fact that both the DST and Toda chains arise from the XXX spin chain in a certain limit. In Appendix
B
we show in detail how this limit works for different objects and equations.

First, we recall the reduction of Lax matrix for XXX spin chain (of spin
s
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+S&S_{-}\\[3.0pt]
S_{+}&u-S\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}u+z\partial_{z}+s&-\partial_{z}\\[3.0pt]
z^{2}\partial_{z}+2sz&u-z\partial_{z}-s\end{pmatrix}

(1.90)

to the Lax matrices for DST and Toda chains, as explained in
[
Skl3
]
.

Second, we consider the reduction of the spin chain
\mathsf{R}
-operator

\bigl[\mathsf{R}_{12}(u_{1}|v_{1},v_{2})\,\Phi\bigr](z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{1}{\Gamma(v_{1}-u_{1})}\,\int_{0}^{1}d\alpha\;\alpha^{v_{1}-u_{1}-1}\,(1-\alpha)^{u_{1}-v_{2}}\\[0.0pt]
\times\Phi(z_{1},(1-\alpha)z_{2}+\alpha z_{1})

(1.91)

and of the Yang–Baxter equation it satisfies to the Toda chain operator
\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)
and the corresponding equation (
1.27
), as well as to DST chain
\mathcal{R}
-operators appearing in Appendix
A
.

At last, we demonstrate that the spin chain
\mathsf{K}
-operator

\bigl[\mathsf{K}(v,s)\,\Phi\bigr](z)=\frac{(2\mathrm{i}\beta)^{-2v}}{\Gamma(-2v)}\,(z+\mathrm{i}\beta)^{g+v-s}\\[3.0pt]
\times\int_{0}^{1}dt\;(1-t)^{-2v-1}\,t^{g+v+s-1}\,\bigl(t(z-\mathrm{i}\beta)+2\mathrm{i}\beta\bigr)^{v+s-g}\,\Phi(t(z-\mathrm{i}\beta)+\mathrm{i}\beta)

(1.92)

and the reflection equation it satisfies can be reduced to the considered previously operator
\mathcal{K}(v)
and the corresponding equation (
1.64
).

These limiting procedures provide one more way to check the formulas discussed before.

1.2.6
Further results

In the second paper in this series
[
BDK
]
, we establish several properties of the raising and Baxter operators introduced here, analogous to those for the
GL
model. First, we establish reflection symmetry and exchange relation

\displaystyle\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(-\lambda),\qquad\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n-1}(\rho)=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\rho)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n-1}(\lambda).

(1.93)

Since eigenfunctions are defined by the formula

\displaystyle\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda_{n})\cdots\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1,

(1.94)

the above properties imply the symmetry in spectral parameters under signed permutations

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\Psi_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}\lambda_{\sigma(n)}}(\bm{x}_{n}),\qquad\varepsilon_{j}\in\{1,-1\},\quad\sigma\in S_{n}.

(1.95)

As a result, the spectra of Hamiltonians (
1.63
) is nondegenerate. Furthermore, this symmetry together with (
1.78
) lead to the formula

\displaystyle D(\pm\lambda_{j})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=-\beta(g\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j})\,\Psi_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{j}\mp\mathrm{i},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}).

(1.96)

These formulas are analogues of (
1.43
) for the
GL
model.

Besides, we prove the commutativity of Baxter operators and the local relation between Baxter and raising operators

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\rho)=\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\rho)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda),

(1.97)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\rho)=\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\lambda-\mathrm{i}\rho)\,\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\lambda+\mathrm{i}\rho)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\rho)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n-1}(\lambda),

(1.98)

where for
n=1
we denote

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{0}(\lambda)=\frac{(2\beta)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}\,\Gamma(2\mathrm{i}\lambda)}{\Gamma(g+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\mathrm{Id}.

(1.99)

From the second relation and definition (
1.94
) we deduce that Hamiltonians’ eigenfunctions also diagonalize Baxter operators

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\frac{(2\beta)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}\,\Gamma(2\mathrm{i}\lambda)}{\Gamma(g+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\lambda-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j})\,\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\lambda+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}).

(1.100)

This formula is in accordance with Baxter equation (
1.86
).

The Baxter operators’ diagonalization property can be then used to derive the so-called
Mellin–Barnes representation
for
BC
Toda eigenfunctions, which generalizes well-known one particle formula
[
DLMF
,
(13.16.12)
]

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{e^{\frac{x}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\beta}}\,W_{\frac{1}{2}-g,-\mathrm{i}\lambda}(2\beta e^{-x})=e^{\beta e^{-x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}-\mathrm{i}0}\frac{d\gamma}{2\pi}\;\frac{(2\beta)^{-\mathrm{i}\gamma}\,\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\gamma-\mathrm{i}\lambda)\,\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\gamma+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}{\Gamma(g+\mathrm{i}\gamma)}\;e^{\mathrm{i}\gamma x}.

(1.101)

For
GL
Toda chain Gauss–Givental and Mellin–Barnes representations combined allow one to prove orthogonality and completeness of the eigenfunctions
[
Sil
,
K
,
DKM
]
, and in the subsequent paper
[
BDK
]
we show that the same is true for
BC
model.

Finally, let us remark that the Whittaker function appearing in one particle case (
1.5
) also admits series representation
[
DLMF
,
(13.14.33)
,
(13.14.6)
]
. Its generalization to the case of arbitrary number of particles is studied in the paper
[
DE
]
. In
[
BDK
]
we present a precise description of such a series.

2
Reflection operator

In this section we show how to derive the following formula for the reflection operator

\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}v}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}-\mathrm{i}v\bigr)}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\,\exp\bigl(-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}\bigr)\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\\
\times\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\,\varphi(-y)

(2.1)

from the reflection equation with DST Lax matrices

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,M^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M(u-v)\sigma_{2}=M(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathcal{K}(v).

(2.2)

Explicitly this equation reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{K}(v)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial&-e^{x}\partial\\[4.0pt]
e^{-x}&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\alpha&u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\\[4.0pt]
-\beta^{2}\bigl(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\bigr)&-\alpha\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&e^{x}\partial\\[4.0pt]
-e^{-x}&u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial\end{array}\right)\\[6.0pt]
&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial&e^{-x}\\[2.0pt]
-e^{x}\partial&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\alpha&u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\\[4.0pt]
-\beta^{2}\bigl(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\bigr)&-\alpha\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&-e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
e^{x}\partial&-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial\end{array}\right)\,\mathcal{K}(v),\end{aligned}

(2.3)

where for brevity we denote
\partial\equiv\partial_{x}
. The derivation consist of two steps: first, we reduce the matrix equation above to three relations, two of which are enough to determine the reflection operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,e^{-x}=e^{x}\partial\,\mathcal{K}(v),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[(\mathrm{i}v+\partial)\,e^{-x}-\beta^{2}\,e^{x}\partial\bigr]=\bigl[(-\mathrm{i}v-\partial)\,e^{x}\partial+2\alpha+\beta^{2}\,e^{-x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v).

(2.5)

The second step is to realize
\mathcal{K}(v)
as an integral operator, rewrite these relations as differential equations for its kernel and solve them. After derivation we check that the solution also satisfies the additional third relation given by (
2.9
).

Remark 5
.

The particular constant behind the integral in (
2.1
) is chosen to simplify various identities with reflection operator. Besides, the gamma function in denominator suits analytic continuation of reflection operator, see the end of this section.

Reducing matrix equation.

The product of matrices from the left hand side of equation (
2.3
) can be
represented in the following form

\displaystyle M^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M(u-v)\sigma_{2}=\mathrm{i}\alpha\,(v-u)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)+\alpha\,(2u-\mathrm{i})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&e^{x}\partial\\
0&0\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(u+v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{-x}&-u^{2}+(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{-2x}&e^{-x}(u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\end{array}\right)+\beta^{2}\left(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial&(e^{x}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
1&-\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial\end{array}\right).

Similarly for the product of matrices from the right hand side

\displaystyle M(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}=\mathrm{i}\alpha\,(v-u)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)+\alpha\,(2u-\mathrm{i})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&e^{-x}\\
0&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{x}\partial&-u^{2}+(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
-(e^{x}\partial)^{2}&e^{x}\partial\,(u+v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\end{array}\right)+\beta^{2}\left(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathrm{i}e^{-x}&e^{-2x}\\[4.0pt]
1&\mathrm{i}e^{-x}\end{array}\right).

As one can see, the first terms with factors
(v-u)
cancel, while all the remaining terms contain factors

(u-\mathrm{i}/2)
, which we remove from both sides. After these transformations we arrive at

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{K}(v)\,\Biggl[2\alpha\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}&e^{x}\partial\\[4.0pt]
0&0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}(u+v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{-x}&-u^{2}+(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{-2x}&e^{-x}(u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\end{pmatrix}+\beta^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial&(e^{x}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
1&-\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial\end{pmatrix}\Biggr]\\[6.0pt]
&=\Biggl[2\alpha\begin{pmatrix}0&e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
0&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}(u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{x}\partial&-u^{2}+(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
-(e^{x}\partial)^{2}&e^{x}\partial\,(u+v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\end{pmatrix}+\beta^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{-x}&e^{-2x}\\[4.0pt]
1&\mathrm{i}e^{-x}\end{pmatrix}\Biggr]\,\mathcal{K}(v).\end{aligned}

(2.6)

The operator
\mathcal{K}(v)
does not depend on
u
, hence, coefficients corresponding to different powers of
u
from both sides should coincide. The equality of coefficients behind
u^{2}
is trivial, while for
u^{1}
one obtains the relation (
2.4
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,e^{-x}=e^{x}\partial\,\mathcal{K}(v).

(2.7)

It remains to consider the matrix relation (
2.6
) at
u=0

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{K}(v)\,\Biggl[2\alpha\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}&e^{x}\partial\\[4.0pt]
0&0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{-x}&(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{-2x}&e^{-x}(-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\end{pmatrix}+\beta^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial&(e^{x}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
1&-\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial\end{pmatrix}\Biggr]\\[6.0pt]
&=\Biggl[2\alpha\begin{pmatrix}0&e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
0&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}(-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{x}\partial&(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}\\[4.0pt]
-(e^{x}\partial)^{2}&e^{x}\partial\,(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\end{pmatrix}+\beta^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{-x}&e^{-2x}\\[4.0pt]
1&\mathrm{i}e^{-x}\end{pmatrix}\Biggr]\,\mathcal{K}(v).\end{aligned}

(2.8)

The equality between
(2,1)
matrix elements

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,e^{-2x}=(e^{x}\partial)^{2}\,\mathcal{K}(v)

is evident consequence of (
2.7
). The remaining relations corresponding to

(1,1)
,
(2,2)
and
(1,2)
matrix elements are as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{-x}+2\mathrm{i}\alpha+\mathrm{i}\beta^{2}\,e^{x}\partial\bigr]=\bigl[(-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{x}\partial-\mathrm{i}\beta^{2}\,e^{-x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[e^{-x}\,(-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)-\mathrm{i}\beta^{2}\,e^{x}\partial\bigr]=\bigl[e^{x}\partial\,(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)+2\mathrm{i}\alpha+\mathrm{i}\beta^{2}\,e^{-x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}+2\alpha\,e^{x}\partial+\beta^{2}\,(e^{x}\partial)^{2}\bigr]=\bigl[(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)^{2}+2\alpha\,e^{-x}+\beta^{2}\,e^{-2x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v).

(2.9)

Notice that the first equation coincides with the announced relation (
2.5
). The second equation is equivalent to the first one due to (
2.7
): one just needs to rewrite the first terms from both sides

\displaystyle e^{-x}\,(-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)=(-v+\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{-x}+\mathrm{i}e^{-x},\qquad e^{x}\partial\,(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)=(v-\mathrm{i}\partial)\,e^{x}\partial+\mathrm{i}e^{x}\partial.

(2.10)

Thus, we reduced the initial matrix equation to three relations. Two of them are enough to determine the explicit form of reflection operator, while the third one (
2.9
) can be checked at the end.

Solving key relations.

To solve two relations

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{K}(v)\,e^{-x}=e^{x}\partial\,\mathcal{K}(v),\\[6.0pt]
&\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[(\mathrm{i}v+\partial)\,e^{-x}-\beta^{2}\,e^{x}\partial\bigr]=\bigl[(-\mathrm{i}v-\partial)\,e^{x}\partial+2\alpha+\beta^{2}\,e^{-x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v)\end{aligned}

(2.11)

we realize
\mathcal{K}(v)
as an integral operator acting on function
\varphi(x)
by the formula

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)=\int_{\mathcal{C}}dy\,K(x,y)\,\varphi(-y).

(2.12)

Moreover, we assume that the contour
\mathcal{C}
and function
\varphi(x)
are such that boundary terms from integration by parts vanish

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\partial\,\varphi\bigr](x)=-\int_{\mathcal{C}}dy\,K(x,y)\,\partial_{y}\varphi(-y)=\int_{\mathcal{C}}dy\,\bigl(\partial_{y}K(x,y)\bigr)\,\varphi(-y).

(2.13)

Then the relations (
2.11
) can be rewritten as equations for the kernel

\displaystyle\partial_{x}K(x,y)=e^{y-x}\,K(x,y),

(2.14)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}K(x,y)=\biggl(1-e^{y-x}+\frac{2\alpha\,e^{-y}-1-2\mathrm{i}v}{1-\beta^{2}\,e^{-2y}}\biggr)K(x,y).

(2.15)

The solution of these differential equations has the form

\displaystyle K(x,y)=A\exp\bigl(-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}\bigr)\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}

(2.16)

where
A
is arbitrary constant. This coincides with the stated expression (
2.1
).

In the same way, integrating by parts one rewrites the third relation (
2.9
) as the equation on the kernel

\displaystyle\Bigl((v-\mathrm{i}\partial_{y})^{2}-(v-\mathrm{i}\partial_{x})^{2}+2\alpha\bigl[\partial_{y}e^{-y}-e^{-x}\bigr]+\beta^{2}\bigl[(\partial_{y}e^{-y})^{2}-e^{-2x}\bigr]\Bigr)K(x,y)=0.

(2.17)

It is straightforward to check that the solution (
2.16
) satisfies this equation as well.

Now let us comment on the choice of integration contour and space of functions in (
2.12
). The kernel
K(x,y)
is fastly decreasing as
y\to\infty
for any
v\in\mathbb{C}
. Besides, it is integrable near
y=\ln\beta
under assumption

\displaystyle\Re\biggl(-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\biggr)>-1\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\Im v>-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}.

(2.18)

Thus, under this condition the action of reflection operator (
2.12
) with integration along the real line
\mathcal{C}=(\ln\beta,\infty)
is well defined on continuous functions
\varphi(x)
that don’t grow too rapidly as
x\to-\infty
.

However, to have vanishing boundary terms after integrating by parts (
2.13
) one needs additional constraints on
\varphi(x)
and the stronger assumption

\displaystyle\Re\biggl(-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\biggr)>0\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\Im v>\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta},

(2.19)

which guarantees that

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\ln\beta^{+}}\,(1-\beta e^{-y})^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}=0.

(2.20)

Furthermore, for the third relation (
2.9
) we need to integrate by parts two times, which makes the necessary assumption on
v
even stronger:
\Im v>3/2-\alpha/\beta
.

The crux is that in analysis of Toda eigenfunctions we need reflection equation with the weakest of all assumptions
\Im v>-1/2-\alpha/\beta
. This leads to the following strategy:

(1)

find nice enough space of functions
\varphi(x)
, such that
[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi](x)
and its derivatives with respect to
x
are analytic in
v
under assumption
\Im v>-1/2-\alpha/\beta
;

(2)

under stronger assumption
\Im v>3/2-\alpha/\beta
prove relations (
2.9
), (
2.11
) (and consequently, reflection equation (
2.2
)) using integration by parts;

(3)

analytically continue these relations to the domain
\Im v>-1/2-\alpha/\beta
at the end.

The details are given in Section
7.3
, and here we only state the result. The suitable space, which is denoted by
\mathcal{E}
, consists of
exponentially tempered
smooth functions
\varphi(x)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, that is for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exist
a,b\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl|\varphi^{(k)}(x)\bigr|\leq a\,e^{b|x|}.

(2.21)

On such space the reflection operator
\mathcal{K}(v)
is well defined and the reflection equation (
2.2
) holds under assumption
\Im v>-1/2-\alpha/\beta
. This concludes the proof of the formula (
2.1
).

Analytic continuation.

Let us mention that the action of reflection operator
\mathcal{K}(v)
can be analytically continued to the whole complex plane
v\in\mathbb{C}
, if we consider functions
\varphi(x)
that are analytic and don’t grow too rapidly in some strip near real line.

y
\ln\beta
\mathcal{H}

Figure 1
:
Hankel contour and branch cut

The kernel
K(x,y)
(
2.16
) has the branch cut along the interval
[\ln\beta,\infty)
. Hence, in the realization of reflection operator (
2.12
) we can alternatively choose Hankel contour
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{H}
, see Figure
1
. If one also chooses the different constant behind the integral, namely

\bigl[\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{an}}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)=-\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}v}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}+\mathrm{i}v\bigr)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\,\int_{\mathcal{H}}dy\;\exp\bigl(-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}\bigr)\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\\
\times\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\,\varphi(-y),

(2.22)

then the corresponding expression represents analytic continuation of the first formula for the reflection operator (
2.1
). Indeed, assuming
\Im v>-1/2-\alpha/\beta
we can standardly pass from the Hankel contour to the branch cut

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\mathcal{H}}dy\,K(x,y)\,\varphi(-y)&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\,\bigl(K(x,y-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon)-K(x,y+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon)\bigr)\,\varphi(-y)\\[6.0pt]
&=2\mathrm{i}\sin\biggl[\pi\biggl(-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr]\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\,K(x,y)\,\varphi(-y).\end{aligned}

(2.23)

From this using the well known formula

\displaystyle\sin\biggl[\pi\biggl(-\mathrm{i}v-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr]=-\frac{\pi}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}-\mathrm{i}v\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}+\mathrm{i}v\bigr)}

(2.24)

we deduce that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{an}}(v)=\mathcal{K}(v)
.

3
Construction of eigenfunctions

By Proposition
6
proven in Section
7.4
, the monodromy operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{n0}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(v)\,\mathcal{K}_{0}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(v)

(3.1)

satisfies reflection equation

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,M_{0}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{0}(u-v)\sigma_{2}=M_{0}(u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{0}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v).

(3.2)

In the simplest case
n=0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{00}(v)=\mathcal{K}_{0}(v),\qquad\mathbb{T}_{0}(u)=K(u).

(3.3)

Explicitly, acting on function
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n},x_{0})
the monodromy operator is given by the formula

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;\mathbb{U}_{v}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n})\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n},-x_{n})

(3.4)

with the kernel

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{U}_{v}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}v}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}v)}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}&d\bm{z}_{n+1}\;\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}v\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}+\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}-2\underline{\bm{z}}_{n+1}\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
&-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(e^{z_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-z_{j+1}}+e^{y_{j}-z_{j+1}})-e^{z_{n+1}-x_{0}}\biggr)\\[6.0pt]
&\times\bigl(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1}\;\theta(z_{1}-\ln\beta).\end{aligned}

(3.5)

The raising operator
\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)
maps functions of
n-1
coordinates
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})
to functions of
n
coordinates. It is defined as a restriction of monodromy operator

\displaystyle\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda x_{n}}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})}.

(3.6)

In this section we demonstrate that eigenfunctions of
BC
Toda chain

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\biggl(u-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\biggr)\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\lambda_{j}^{2}-u^{2})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})

(3.7)

can be constructed using raising operators

\displaystyle\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda_{n})\cdots\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cdot 1.

(3.8)

For this we show that raising operator satisfies the following relation with
\mathbb{B}
-operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{2}-u^{2})\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{B}_{n-1}(u).

(3.9)

Note that by definition
\mathbb{B}_{0}=(u-\mathrm{i}/2)
. Thus, the latter relation applied to the function (
3.8
) leads to the formula (
3.7
).

To prove (
3.9
) we write DST matrices in the relation (
3.2
) explicitly

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&-e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\\[3.0pt]
e^{-x_{n}}&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\\[3.0pt]
-e^{-x_{n}}&u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{array}\right)\\[6.0pt]
&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
-e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&-e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}&-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{array}\right)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v).\end{aligned}

(3.10)

First, let us extract the equality of
(1,2)
matrix elements. That is consider the first row
from the most left matrix and the second column from the most
right matrix

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\begin{pmatrix}-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&-e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\\[3.0pt]
u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\\[6.0pt]
&=\begin{pmatrix}u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&e^{-x_{n}}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v).\end{aligned}

(3.11)

Next transform the row and column in the right hand side

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&e^{-x_{n}}\end{pmatrix}=e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&e^{-x_{n}}\end{pmatrix}\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}=e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}.

(3.13)

Note that the transformed row coincides with the first row of the matrix
L_{n}(u)
, while transformed column coincides with the second column of the matrix
\sigma_{2}\,L^{t}_{n}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle L_{n}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
-e^{x_{n}}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{2}\,L^{t}_{n}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
e^{x_{n}}&-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.14)

Besides, recall that by definition

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\sigma_{2}\,L^{t}_{n}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{A}_{n}(u)&\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}_{n}(u)&\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\end{pmatrix}.

(3.15)

Hence, after transformations in the right hand side of the formula (
3.11
) we obtain the operator
\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)

\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\begin{pmatrix}-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}&-e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\\[3.0pt]
u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\\[6.0pt]
=e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v).

(3.16)

If we apply this operator identity to the function which does not depend on
x_{n}
, then derivatives
\partial_{n}
from the left disappear

\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\begin{pmatrix}-u-v&0\end{pmatrix}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{A}_{n-1}(u)&\mathbb{B}_{n-1}(u)\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}_{n-1}(u)&\mathbb{D}_{n-1}(u)\end{array}\right)\begin{pmatrix}0\\[3.0pt]
u-v\end{pmatrix}\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})\\[6.0pt]
=e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1}).

(3.17)

Simplifying the left hand side we arrive at the formula

\displaystyle(v^{2}-u^{2})\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\mathbb{B}_{n-1}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})=\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1}),

(3.18)

which coincides with the stated relation (
3.9
).

Finally, let us remark that the initial matrix relation (
3.10
) holds on the space of exponentially tempered smooth functions
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, see Proposition
6
. Hence, the same is true for the intertwining relation (
3.9
). In Section
7.2
we prove that the integral representation (
3.8
) is absolutely convergent and derive estimate for it (Proposition
1
). This estimate, in particular, implies that the eigenfunctions belong to the space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(Corollary
7
), which justifies the construction above.

4
Commutativity of Baxter operators and Hamiltonians

Baxter operator is related to the monodromy operator (
3.1
) through the restriction and limit

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)=\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\mathbb{U}_{n0}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})}.

(4.1)

Explicitly, it is given by the integral operator

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;\mathbb{Q}_{\lambda}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n})\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n})

(4.2)

with the kernel

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{Q}_{\lambda}(\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{y}_{n})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}&d\bm{z}_{n+1}\;\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}+\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}-2\underline{\bm{z}}_{n+1}\bigr)\\[6.0pt]
&-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(e^{z_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-z_{j+1}}+e^{y_{j}-z_{j+1}})\biggr)\\[6.0pt]
&\times\bigl(1+\beta e^{-z_{1}}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-z_{1}}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+g-1}\;\theta(z_{1}-\ln\beta),\end{aligned}

(4.3)

which is convergent under assumption
\Im\lambda\in(-g,0)
. The explicit expression for the kernel follows from the formula (
3.5
). The interchange of limit and integration is justified for a suitable space of functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})
, see Corollary
5
in Section
7.5
.

In this section we prove that Baxter operators commute with Hamiltonians

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)=\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u).

(4.4)

First, recall the intertwining relation for the monodromy operator (
3.2
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{0}&-e^{x_{0}}\partial_{0}\\[3.0pt]
e^{-x_{0}}&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&e^{x_{0}}\partial_{0}\\[3.0pt]
-e^{-x_{0}}&u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{0}\end{array}\right)\\[6.0pt]
&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{0}&e^{-x_{0}}\\[3.0pt]
-e^{x_{0}}\partial_{0}&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}&-e^{-x_{0}}\\[3.0pt]
e^{x_{0}}\partial_{0}&-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{0}\end{array}\right)\,\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v).\end{aligned}

(4.5)

Consider the equality of
(1,2)
elements and act from both sides on function
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})
, so that the derivatives
\partial_{0}
from the left disappear

(-u-v)(u-v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\\[6.0pt]
=\begin{pmatrix}u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{0}&e^{-x_{0}}\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{A}_{n}(u)&\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}_{n}(u)&\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{0}}\\[3.0pt]
-u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{0}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n}).

(4.6)

Next we take the limit
x_{0}\to\infty
. The exponents
e^{-x_{0}}
from the right vanish. Besides, the explicit formula for the kernel of monodromy operator (
3.5
) suggests the equality

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\partial_{0}\,\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})=0.

(4.7)

It is proven in Section
7.5
for a suitable space of functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})
, see Corollary
5
. Thus, in the limit
x_{0}\to\infty
it is possible to remove
e^{-x_{0}}
and
\partial_{0}
from the right hand side of (
4.6
), so that this relation is reduced to the needed form

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})=\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n}).

(4.8)

The technical details about the spaces of functions, on which all above relations hold are given in Section
7.5
. Namely, by Corollary
5
Baxter operators are well defined on the space of exponentially tempered smooth functions with at most polynomial growth as
x_{n}\to\infty
, which we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover, Baxter operator maps this space to
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, while the operator
\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)
acts invariantly on it, see Corollary
6
.

5
Baxter equation

In this section we derive the Baxter equation

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)=-\frac{\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)}{2u}\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(u-\mathrm{i}).

(5.1)

The derivation consists of two parts. First, we rewrite some known local relations with
\mathcal{R}
- and
\mathcal{K}
-operators in a suitable form. Second, we use them to perform reduction of a certain global relation with monodromy operator and monodromy matrix.

Rewriting local relations.

To begin, we rewrite intertwining relation (
1.27
)

\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,M_{2}(u-v)=M_{2}(u-v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)

(5.2)

in an equivalent form

\displaystyle U^{-1}_{2}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{2}=V_{2}(u-v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,V^{-1}_{2}(u-v)

(5.3)

using factorized expression for the matrix
M(\lambda)

\displaystyle M(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}&e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x}\partial_{x}&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}=U\,V(\lambda),

(5.4)

where

\displaystyle U=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad V(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x}\partial_{x}&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.5)

Next with the help of explicit expression for the operator
\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)
(
1.28
) we rewrite the relation (
5.3
) as follows

\displaystyle U^{-1}_{2}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}(u-v)\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}_{12}(v-\mathrm{i})&-\mathrm{i}(u-v)\,e^{-x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\\[6.0pt]
-e^{x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v+\mathrm{i})&\mathrm{i}e^{x_{21}}\partial_{2}\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\end{pmatrix},

(5.6)

so that at the point
v=u
one obtains

\displaystyle U^{-1}_{2}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(u)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}_{12}(u-\mathrm{i})&0\\[6.0pt]
-e^{x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(u+\mathrm{i})&\mathrm{i}e^{x_{21}}\partial_{2}\mathcal{R}_{12}(u)\end{pmatrix}.

(5.7)

Here for brevity we denote
x_{21}\equiv x_{2}-x_{1}
.
Similarly for the operator
\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(u)
(
1.69
) we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}^{-1}_{2}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\,\widetilde{U}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{x_{2}+x_{1}}\partial_{2}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(u)&-e^{-x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(u+\mathrm{i})\\[6.0pt]
0&\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(u-\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix}

(5.8)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}=\sigma_{2}U\sigma_{2},\qquad\widetilde{L}(u)=\sigma_{2}L^{t}(-u)\sigma_{2}.

(5.9)

The same factorization (
5.4
) can be used to transform the
reflection equation (
2.2
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,M^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M(u-v)\sigma_{2}=M(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathcal{K}(v)

(5.10)

to the form

\displaystyle U^{-1}\,\mathcal{K}(v)\,M^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}=V(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\,\mathcal{K}(v)\,\sigma_{2}V^{-1}(u-v)\sigma_{2}.

(5.11)

Notice that in the right hand side we have singularity at
v=u

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}V^{-1}(u-v)\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}&\frac{\mathrm{i}}{u-v}e^{x}\partial_{x}\\[6.0pt]
0&\frac{1}{u-v}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.12)

The last relation can be rewritten as

U^{-1}\,\mathcal{K}(v)\,M^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}\\[6.0pt]
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\[6.0pt]
-\frac{e^{x}\partial_{x}}{u-v}&\frac{\mathrm{i}}{u-v}\end{pmatrix}\,K(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}(u-v)\mathcal{K}(v)&-\mathcal{K}(v)\,e^{x}\partial_{x}-e^{-x}\,\mathcal{K}(v)\\[6.0pt]
-\mathrm{i}(u-v)e^{x}\partial_{x}\mathcal{K}(v)&\mathrm{i}\bigl(\partial_{x}\mathcal{K}(v)+\mathcal{K}(v)\partial_{x}\bigr)-(u+v)\mathcal{K}(v)\end{pmatrix}.

(5.13)

Consider only the first row of this matrix equality and put
v=u

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}\,U^{-1}\,\mathcal{K}(u)\,M^{t}(-2u)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}\\
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,K(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathcal{K}(u)\,e^{x}\partial_{x}-e^{-x}\,\mathcal{K}(u)\\[6.0pt]
0&\mathrm{i}\bigl(\partial_{x}\mathcal{K}(u)+\mathcal{K}(u)\partial_{x}\bigr)-2u\,\mathcal{K}(u)\end{pmatrix}.

(5.14)

Then using explicit formula for the reflection operator (
2.1
) we can rewrite the right hand side in a simpler form

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}\,U^{-1}\,\mathcal{K}(u)\,M^{t}(-2u)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}=\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)\,\mathcal{K}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(5.15)

The derivation of the Baxter equation is based on the obtained local relations (
5.7
), (
5.8
) and (
5.15
).

Reducing global relation.

Recall definitions of the monodromy operator and matrix

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{n0}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(v)\,\mathcal{K}_{0}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(v),\\[6.0pt]
&\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\cdots L_{1}(u)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\widetilde{L}_{n}(u).\end{aligned}

(5.16)

Using them together with the commutation relation (
1.68
)

\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,M^{t}_{2}(-u-v)\,L_{1}(u)=L_{1}(u)\,M^{t}_{2}(-u-v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)

(5.17)

we arrive at the following global relation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{U}_{n0}(v)\,M_{0}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)&=\mathcal{R}_{n0}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\\[6.0pt]
&\times\mathcal{K}_{0}(v)\,M_{0}^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\,\cdots\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u).\end{aligned}

(5.18)

Now consider
(1,2)
element of the matrix from the left, put
v=u
and perform corresponding reductions to obtain Baxter operator (
4.1
)

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\mathbb{U}_{n0}(u)\begin{pmatrix}-2u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{0}}&-e^{x_{0}}\partial_{x_{0}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\\[4.0pt]
\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\end{pmatrix}\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})=-2u\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n}).

(5.19)

The last product is as needed in the Baxter equation (
5.1
)

Next step is to extract the same matrix element from the right hand side (
5.18
), but before let us divide it into three parts

\Bigl[\mathcal{R}_{n0}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{0}\Bigr]\\[6.0pt]
\times\Bigl[U_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{K}_{0}(v)\,M_{0}^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}_{0}\Bigr]\,\Bigl[\widetilde{U}_{0}^{-1}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\,\cdots\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\Bigr],

(5.20)

and transform these parts separately using known local relations.

First, using local relation (
5.7
) we rewrite the first product in square brackets

U_{0}\,U^{-1}_{0}\,\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u)\,L_{n}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(u)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}e^{-x_{0}}\\[4.0pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix}\\[6.0pt]
\times\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u-\mathrm{i})&0\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{n}}\,\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u+\mathrm{i})&\mathrm{i}e^{x_{0n}}\partial_{0}\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u)\end{pmatrix}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}_{10}(u-\mathrm{i})&0\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{10}(u+\mathrm{i})&\mathrm{i}e^{x_{01}}\partial_{0}\mathcal{R}_{10}(u)\end{pmatrix}.

(5.21)

In the limit
x_{0}\to\infty
the exponent
e^{-x_{0}}
vanishes, so that one obtains the following first row

\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u)\,L_{n}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(u)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{0}\\
=\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\,(-\mathrm{i})^{n}\,\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(5.22)

Since the result is proportional to the vector
(1\;\;0)
, in the second product in square brackets (
5.20
) we also need to extract only the first row. This allows us to use local relation (
5.15
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}\,U^{-1}_{0}\,\mathcal{K}_{0}(u)\,M^{t}_{0}(-2u)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}_{0}=\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)\,\mathcal{K}_{0}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(5.23)

Again, since the result is proportional to the vector
(0\;\;1)
, in the last product in square brackets (
5.20
) we only need
(2,2)
entry. Using the local relation (
5.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&1\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{U}^{-1}_{0}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{U}_{0}\,\widetilde{U}^{-1}_{0}\,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\mathrm{i}^{n}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(u-\mathrm{i}).

(5.24)

Collecting everything together we arrive at the following relation

-2u\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\\[6.0pt]
=\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)\lim_{x_{0}\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{n0}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}_{10}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\mathcal{K}_{0}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{10}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n0}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n}),

(5.25)

which is equivalent to the announced Baxter equation (
5.1
).

In full analogy with the previous section, all relations used here hold on the space
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The only subtle point is that the spectral parameter of the Baxter operator
\mathbb{Q}_{n}(u)
should satisfy
\Im u\in(-g,0)
, see Corollary
5
. Hence, the appearance of
\mathbb{Q}_{n}(u-\mathrm{i})
on the right requires a stronger condition
\Im u\in(1-g,0)
, which is non-empty only if
g>1
.

6
Action of
\mathbb{D}(u)
on raising operator

With a minor modification of the derivation from the previous section, we can compute the action of
\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)
on the raising operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(u)=-\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(u-\mathrm{i}).

(6.1)

We start with the equality (
3.2
)

M_{n}(u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{n}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\\[6.0pt]
=\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,M_{n}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{n}(u-v)\sigma_{2}.

(6.2)

Next we insert definitions of monodromy operator and monodromy matrix (
5.16
) from the right and use intertwining relation (
5.17
) to obtain

M_{n}(u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{n}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\\[6.0pt]
=\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(v)\,L_{n-1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)\,M^{t}_{n}(-u-v)\,K(u)\\[6.0pt]
\times\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n-1}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{n}(u-v)\sigma_{2}.

(6.3)

Multiplying both sides by the vector

\displaystyle e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\begin{pmatrix}-e^{x_{n}}&0\end{pmatrix}M_{n}^{-1}(u-v)=\frac{e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}}{\mathrm{i}(u-v)}\,\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n}}&1\end{pmatrix}

(6.4)

we arrive at the equality

\begin{pmatrix}-e^{x_{n}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,\sigma_{2}M_{n}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\\[6.0pt]
=e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\begin{pmatrix}-e^{x_{n}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}M^{-1}_{n}(u-v)\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(v)\,L_{n-1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)\,M^{t}_{n}(-u-v)\,K(u)\\[6.0pt]
\times\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n-1}(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{n}(u-v)\sigma_{2},

(6.5)

or writing all matrices explicitly

\begin{pmatrix}-e^{x_{n}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}&-e^{-x_{n}}\\[4.0pt]
e^{x_{n}}(\partial_{n}-\mathrm{i}v)&-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\\[6.0pt]
=\frac{e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}}{\mathrm{i}(u-v)}\,\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n}}&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(v)\,L_{n-1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)\,M^{t}_{n}(-u-v)\,K(u)\\[3.0pt]
\times\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}&e^{x_{n}}\partial_{n}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{n}}&u-v+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(6.6)

Now we multiply both sides from the right by the vector
(0\;\;1)^{t}
and act on function
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})
, so that the derivatives
\partial_{n}
from the right disappear

\begin{pmatrix}-e^{x_{n}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{n}}\\[4.0pt]
-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})\\[6.0pt]
=-\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n}}&1\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(v)\,L_{n-1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)\,M^{t}_{n}(-u-v)\,K(u)\\[6.0pt]
\times\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1}).

(6.7)

By definitions,

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}-e^{x_{n}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{n}}\\[4.0pt]
-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(v)=e^{\mathrm{i}vx_{n}}\mathbb{U}_{n-1,n}(v)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})},

(6.8)

so the left hand side of the last expression at the point
