v=u
is as desired (
6.1
).
Besides, in the right hand side the same mechanism applies as in the derivation of the Baxter equation. First, putting
v=u
due to (
5.7
) we have

\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n}}&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(u)\,L_{n-1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(u)\,L_{1}(u)\,U_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n}}&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}e^{-x_{n}}\\[4.0pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix}\\[2.0pt]
\times\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(u-\mathrm{i})&0\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{n-1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(u+\mathrm{i})&\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n-1\,n}}\partial_{0}\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(u)\end{pmatrix}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{R}_{1n}(u-\mathrm{i})&0\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{1n}(u+\mathrm{i})&\mathrm{i}e^{x_{n1}}\partial_{n}\mathcal{R}_{1n}(u)\end{pmatrix}\\[8.0pt]
=(-\mathrm{i})^{n}\,e^{x_{n}}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(6.9)

This leads to the selection of the first row in the next product containing
reflection operator, which allows to put
v=u
and use relation (
5.15
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\,U^{-1}_{n}\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)\,M^{t}_{n}(-2u)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{U}_{n}=\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(6.10)

Consequently, from the product with
\mathcal{R}^{*}
-operators in the last line (
6.7
) we only need
(2,2)
element. Due to (
5.8
) it equals

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{U}^{-1}_{n}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{U}_{n-1}\,\widetilde{U}^{-1}_{n-1}\,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\\[6.0pt]
=\mathrm{i}^{n-1}\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(u-\mathrm{i}).

(6.11)

Collecting everything together we obtain the following relation

\mathbb{D}_{n}(u)\,\reflectbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\mathbb V$}}}_{n}(u)\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1})=-\beta(g+\mathrm{i}u)\,e^{\mathrm{i}(u-\mathrm{i})x_{n}}\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,n}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1n}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\mathcal{K}_{n}(u-\mathrm{i})\\[6.0pt]
\times\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1n}(u-\mathrm{i})\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{n-1,n}(u-\mathrm{i})\,\varphi(\bm{x}_{n-1}),

(6.12)

which coincides with the desired formula (
6.1
).

At last, let us remark that the initial equality and all used intertwining relations hold on the space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
assuming
\Im u>1-g
, see Sections
7.3
–
7.4
.

7
Bounds

In this section we derive bounds on eigenfunctions and study spaces of functions, on which the operators considered in the paper are well defined and relations between them hold true.

7.1
Lemmas

-4
-2
2
4
0.5
1
y

Figure 2
:
Graph of
e^{-e^{y}}

The idea behind the following two lemmas (which we borrow from
[
BC
, Section 4.1.1]
) is that double exponent
e^{-e^{y}}
is a smooth version of step function
\theta(-y)
, see Figure
2
.

Lemma 1
.

Let
s>0
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\geq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{s-1}\,e^{\kappa y-e^{y+x}}\leq C(s,\kappa)\,e^{-e^{x}},

(7.1)

and convergence of this integral is uniform in
x\geq 0
.

Proof.

The bound can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{s-1}\,e^{\kappa y-e^{x}(e^{y}-1)}\leq C(s,\kappa).

(7.2)

Since
e^{x}\geq 1
and
e^{y}-1\geq y^{2}/2
for
y\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{s-1}\,e^{\kappa y-e^{x}(e^{y}-1)}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{s-1}\,e^{\kappa y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}}.

(7.3)

The last integral is convergent due to assumption
s>0
. Besides, convergence of initial integral is uniform in
x
due to inequality
e^{-e^{y+x}}\leq e^{-e^{y}}
.
∎

Lemma 2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\kappa\geq 0
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,\exp\bigl(\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}\bigr)\\
\leq P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\;\exp\biggl(\biggl[\kappa\,\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}\biggr]\,\theta(x_{1}-x_{2})\biggr),

(7.4)

where
P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\equiv P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|;m,\kappa)
is polynomial in
|x_{j}|
. Convergence of this integral is uniform in
x_{1},x_{2}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

First, consider the case
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
. We need to prove that the integral is bounded by polynomial

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\leq P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|).

(7.5)

Let us divide it into three parts

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy=\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}}dy+\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}dy+\int_{x_{2}}^{\infty}dy,

(7.6)

which we estimate separately. In the first term use inequality
e^{-e^{y-x_{2}}}\leq 1
and change integration variable to
z=x_{1}-y

\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\\[6.0pt]
=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;|x_{1}-z|^{m}\,e^{\kappa z-e^{z}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;(|x_{1}|+z)^{m}\,e^{\kappa z-e^{z}}.

(7.7)

The last integral is polynomial in
|x_{1}|
, since expanding brackets we obtain integrals of the type

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;z^{\ell}\,e^{\kappa z-e^{z}}<\infty.

(7.8)

Besides, its convergence is clearly uniform in
x_{1},x_{2}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

The third integral from (
7.6
) is bounded by polynomial in
|x_{2}|
in similar way (notice that
e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)}\leq e^{\kappa(x_{1}-x_{2})}\leq 1
for
y\geq x_{2}
) and it also converges uniformly in
x_{1},x_{2}
.
At last, the second integral from (
7.6
) can be estimated in the following way

\displaystyle\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}dy\;|y|^{m}\leq(x_{2}-x_{1})|x_{2}|^{m},

(7.9)

which concludes the proof of the inequality (
7.5
).

Next, consider
x_{1}\geq x_{2}
. In this case we need to show that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\leq P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\;e^{\kappa\,\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}}.

(7.10)

For this split the integral into two parts

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy=\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}}dy+\int_{\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}}^{\infty}dy.

(7.11)

In the first one use inequality
e^{-e^{y-x_{2}}}\leq 1
and change integration variable to
z=(x_{1}+x_{2})/2-y

\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\\[6.0pt]
=e^{\kappa\,\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;\biggl|\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}-z\biggr|^{m}\,e^{\kappa z-e^{z+\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}}.

(7.12)

Since
x_{1}\geq x_{2}
, we can use Lemma
1
to bound integrals of the type

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;z^{\ell}\,e^{\kappa z-e^{z+\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}}\leq C(\ell,\kappa)\,e^{-e^{\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}},

(7.13)

which appear after expanding brackets in (
7.12
). Thus, we estimated the first term from (
7.11
) in the desired way, and same arguments can be applied to the second one. Finally, for
x_{1},x_{2}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
we have
|x_{1}\pm x_{2}|\leq C_{\pm}
, which makes convergence of the above integrals uniform in
x_{1},x_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\geq 0
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,\exp\bigl(\kappa_{1}(x_{1}-y)+\kappa_{2}(y-x_{2})-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}\bigr)\\
\leq P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\;\exp\biggl(\biggl[(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2})\,\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{2}}\biggr]\,\theta(x_{1}-x_{2})\biggr),

(7.14)

where
P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\equiv P(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|;m,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})
is polynomial in
|x_{j}|
, and convergence of this integral is uniform in
x_{1},x_{2}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

First, consider the case
\kappa_{1}\geq\kappa_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} e^{\kappa_{1}(x_{1}-y)+\kappa_{2}(y-x_{2})}&=e^{\kappa_{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})+(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2})(x_{1}-y)}\\[6.0pt]
&\leq e^{\kappa_{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})\,\theta(x_{1}-x_{2})+(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2})(x_{1}-y)},\end{aligned}

(7.15)

where the last inequality is due to assumption
\kappa_{2}\geq 0
. Applying it for the integral in question we arrive at

\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{\kappa_{1}(x_{1}-y)+\kappa_{2}(y-x_{2})-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\\
\leq e^{\kappa_{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})\,\theta(x_{1}-x_{2})}\;\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;|y|^{m}\,e^{(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2})(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}.

(7.16)

The last integral can be bounded using Lemma
2
, which gives the stated estimate.

The case
\kappa_{1}\leq\kappa_{2}
reduces to the previous one after the change of variable
y=x_{1}+x_{2}-z
and brackets expansion in

\displaystyle|y|^{m}=|x_{1}+x_{2}-z|^{m}\leq(|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|+|z|)^{m}.

(7.17)

This concludes the proof of corollary.
∎

Recall
GL
Toda raising operator

\displaystyle\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{y}_{n-1}\;\exp\biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}-\underline{\bm{y}}_{n-1}\bigr)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{x_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{y_{j}-x_{j+1}})\biggr)\,\varphi(\bm{y}_{n-1}).

(7.18)

Note that its kernel consists of functions we encountered in previous statements. Let us introduce the space of continuous polynomially bounded functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n}=\bigl\{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\in C(\mathbb{R}^{n})\colon\quad|\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})|\leq P(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|),\quad\text{$P$ --- polynomial}\bigr\}.

(7.19)

The following corollary says that the raising operator acts “invariantly” on this space modulo increasing the number of variables. This statement is a weaker version of
[
BC
, Proposition 4.1.3]
.

Corollary 3
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{P}_{n-1}
. Then
\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Proof.

From definition (
7.18
) and bound on
\varphi\in\mathcal{P}_{n-1}
we have

\displaystyle\Bigl|\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})\Bigr|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{y}_{n-1}\;\exp\biggl(-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{x_{j}-y_{j}}+e^{y_{j}-x_{j+1}})\biggr)\,P(|y_{1}|,\dots,|y_{n-1}|).

(7.20)

Writing polynomial in terms of monomials
|y_{1}|^{m_{1}}\cdots|y_{n-1}|^{m_{n-1}}
we obtain sum of factorised integrals

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy_{j}\;|y_{j}|^{m_{j}}\,\exp\bigl(-e^{x_{j}-y_{j}}-e^{y_{j}-x_{j+1}}\bigr).

(7.21)

By Lemma
2
they are polynomially bounded, which gives the desired estimate

\displaystyle\Bigl|\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})\Bigr|\leq\widetilde{P}(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|).

(7.22)

Moreover, by the same lemma the above integrals converge uniformly in
x_{j}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
, which makes the function
\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(\lambda)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n})
continuous in
\bm{x}_{n}
.
∎

0
2
4
6
8
0
0.5
1
1.5
y
\;g=0.5
\;g=1
\;g=2
\;g=10

Figure 3
:
Graphs of
(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}

In
BC
Toda chain we also encounter function
(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}
with
y,g>0
, which is pictured in Figure
3
. If
g\geq 1
, then it also represents smooth version of step function, whereas in the case
0<g<1
we should take into account that it grows as
1/y^{1-g}
for small
y
.

Lemma 3
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
g>0
,
\kappa\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\,\exp\bigl(\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}\bigr)\\
\leq P(|x|)\,\exp\bigl(\bigl[\kappa x-e^{x}\bigr]\,\theta(x)\bigr),

(7.23)

where
P(|x|)\equiv P(|x|;m,g,\kappa)
is polynomial in
|x|
. Convergence of this integral is uniform in
x
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The case
g\geq 1
is simple, since we can use inequality

\displaystyle(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\leq 1

(7.24)

to write the bound

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}.

(7.25)

Next, if
x\geq 0
, then the claim (
7.23
) follows from Lemma
1
. If
x\leq 0
, then we split the above integral into two parts

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy=\int_{0}^{-x}dy\;+\,\int_{-x}^{\infty}dy.

(7.26)

These parts are bounded by polynomials in
|x|

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0}^{-x}dy\;y^{m}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq\int_{0}^{-x}dy\;y^{m}=\frac{|x|^{m+1}}{m+1},\\[6.0pt]
&\begin{aligned} &\int_{-x}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;(z-x)^{m}\,e^{\kappa z-e^{z}}\leq P(|x|),\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

(7.27)

where the last inequality follows from expanding brackets.

It remains to analyse the case
0<g<1
. We still have
(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\leq 1
, so that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,(1+e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}},

(7.28)

but one needs to take care of the possible singularity at
y=0
. Since

\displaystyle y\in[0,1]\colon

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt1-e^{-y}\geq y-\frac{y^{2}}{2}\geq\frac{y}{2},

(7.29)

\displaystyle y\in[1,\infty)\colon

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt1-e^{-y}\geq 1-e^{-1},

(7.30)

we can divide the integral from the right (
7.28
) into two parts and estimate them separately

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dy\;y^{m}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq 2^{1-g}\,\int_{0}^{1}dy\;y^{m+g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}},

(7.31)

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,(1-e^{-y})^{g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq(1-e^{-1})^{g-1}\,\int_{1}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}.

(7.32)

First, suppose
x\geq 0
. Then for both of the above integrals we can use Lemma
1
. Indeed, consider the first integral

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dy\;y^{m+g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m+g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq C\,e^{\kappa x-e^{x}}.

(7.33)

Similarly for the second one. This gives us the desired bound (
7.23
) in the case
x\geq 0
.

In the remaining case
x\leq 0
for the first integral we just write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dy\;y^{m+g-1}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq e^{\kappa}\int_{0}^{1}dy\;y^{m+g-1}=\frac{e^{\kappa}}{m+g},

(7.34)

whereas for the second one we again use inequalities (
7.27
) to bound it by polynomial in
|x|

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{m}\,e^{\kappa(y+x)-e^{y+x}}\leq P(|x|).

(7.35)

At last, it easy to check that above estimates imply convergence uniform in
x
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.
This concludes the proof of lemma.
∎

7.2
Bounds on eigenfunctions

7.2.1
One particle

The eigenfunction in the case
n=1
is given by the formula

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;\,e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda(x-2y)-e^{y-x}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+g-1},

(7.36)

where
\beta>0
and
g=1/2+\alpha/\beta>0
. For simplicity, in what follows we assume that
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly, the integral in this case is absolutely convergent, but we need some bound on it to deal with the cases
n\geq 2
.

Recall that eigenfunction solves the equation

\displaystyle\bigl(-\partial_{x}^{2}+2\alpha\,e^{-x}+\beta^{2}\,e^{-2x}\bigr)\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda^{2}\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x).

(7.37)

Hence, we expect that it decays as
x\to-\infty
. The following bound suits this expectation.

Lemma 4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\lambda,x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\Psi_{\lambda}(x)
is smooth in
x
and admits the bound

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{x}^{k}\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x)\bigr|\leq\frac{P(|x|,|\lambda|)}{|\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)|}\,e^{[k(\ln\beta-x)-\beta e^{-x}]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x)},

(7.38)

where
P
is polynomial, whose coefficients depend on
k,\beta,g
. In particular, for
k=0
it doesn’t depend on
\lambda

\displaystyle\bigl|\Psi_{\lambda}(x)\bigr|\leq\frac{P(|x|)}{|\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)|}\,e^{-\beta e^{-x}\,\theta(\ln\beta-x)}.

(7.39)

Proof.

Denote the integrand from (
7.36
) as

\displaystyle F(x,y)=e^{\mathrm{i}\lambda(x-2y)-e^{y-x}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda+g-1}.

(7.40)

Its
k
-th derivative is the sum

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{k}\,F(x,y)=\sum_{i}p_{i}(\lambda)\,e^{\ell_{i}(y-x)}\,F(x,y)

(7.41)

with polynomials
p_{i}
and integers
0\leq\ell_{i}\leq k
. Since
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;\bigl|\partial_{x}^{k}\,F(x,y)\bigr|&\leq\sum_{i}|p_{i}|\;\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;e^{\ell_{i}(y-x)-e^{y-x}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y})^{-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y})^{g-1}\\[6.0pt]
&=\sum_{i}|p_{i}|\;\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;e^{\ell_{i}(z+\ln\beta-x)-e^{z+\ln\beta-x}}\,(1+e^{-z})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-z})^{g-1},\end{aligned}

(7.42)

where passing to the last line we changed integration variable
y=z+\ln\beta
. By Lemma
3
integrals in the last sum converge uniformly in
x
and are bounded as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;e^{\ell_{i}(z+\ln\beta-x)-e^{z+\ln\beta-x}}\,(1+e^{-z})^{-g}\,(1-e^{-z})^{g-1}\leq P(|x|)\,e^{[\ell_{i}(\ln\beta-x)-\beta e^{-x}]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x)},

(7.43)

where
P
is polynomial. Furthermore, since
0\leq\ell_{i}\leq k

\displaystyle e^{\ell_{i}(\ln\beta-x)\,\theta(\ln\beta-x)}\leq e^{k(\ln\beta-x)\,\theta(\ln\beta-x)}.

(7.44)

Thus, we arrive at the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;\bigl|\partial_{x}^{k}\,F(x,y)\bigr|\leq P(|x|,|\lambda|)\,e^{[k(\ln\beta-x)-\beta e^{-x}]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x)}.

(7.45)

Since for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the function
\partial_{x}^{k}\,F(x,y)
is continuous in
x,y
and convergence of the above integral is uniform in
x
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
, we can interchange derivative and integral

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{k}\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\partial_{x}^{k}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;F(x,y)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;\partial_{x}^{k}\,F(x,y).

(7.46)

Hence, the estimate (
7.45
) also holds for
\bigl|\partial_{x}^{k}\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x)\bigr|
.

Finally, note that in the case
k=0
we simply have

\displaystyle\bigl|\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda)\,\Psi_{\lambda}(x)\bigr|\leq\Gamma(g)\,\Psi_{0}(x).

(7.47)

This leads to the bound (
7.39
), where polynomial doesn’t depend on
\lambda
.
∎

7.2.2
Many particles

The eigenfunction for arbitrary
n
admits representation

\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n})}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{z}_{n-1}\;\exp\Biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}+\underline{\bm{z}}_{n-1}-2\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}\bigr)\\[4.0pt]
-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-y_{j+1}}+e^{y_{j}-z_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j+1}})-e^{y_{n}-x_{n}}\Biggr)\\[4.0pt]
\times(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}+g-1}\,\theta(y_{1}-\ln\beta)\;\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}(\bm{z}_{n-1}).

(7.48)

Since it solves the equation

\displaystyle\Biggl(-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}e^{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}+2\alpha e^{-x_{1}}+\beta^{2}e^{-2x_{1}}\Biggr)\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}^{2}\Biggr)\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n}),

(7.49)

we expect it to decay if
x_{1}\to-\infty
or
x_{j}-x_{j+1}\to\infty
for some
j
. Proposition
1
suits this expectation. In what follows for
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
we use the standard notation

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}.

(7.50)

Also, for brevity, we denote

\displaystyle C(\bm{\lambda}_{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{j})|}.

(7.51)

Proposition 1
.

Let
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
and
\bm{x}_{n},\bm{\lambda}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
is smooth in
\bm{x}_{n}
and admits the bound

\Bigl|\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})\Bigr|\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n})\,P(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|,|\lambda_{n}|)\,\exp\Biggl(\bigl[k_{1}(\ln\beta-x_{1})-\beta e^{-x_{1}}\bigr]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})\\[2.0pt]
+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\biggl[(k_{j}+k_{j+1})\,\frac{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}{2}}\biggr]\,\theta(x_{j}-x_{j+1})\Biggr),

(7.52)

where
P
is polynomial, whose coefficients depend on
\bm{k}_{n},\beta,g
. In particular, for
\bm{k}_{n}=(0,\dots,0)
it doesn’t depend on
\lambda_{n}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigl|\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|&\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n})\,P(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|)\\[6.0pt]
&\times\exp\Biggl(-\beta e^{-x_{1}}\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}e^{\frac{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}{2}}\,\theta(x_{j}-x_{j+1})\Biggr).\end{aligned}

(7.53)

Proof.

The case
n=1
coincides with Lemma
4
. Let us proceed by induction assuming that we proved proposition for
n-1
particles. Then in recursive formula (
7.48
) we, in particular, have

\displaystyle\bigl|\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}(\bm{z}_{n-1})\bigr|\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n-1})\,P(|z_{1}|,\dots,|z_{n-1}|).

(7.54)

Now consider the result of integration over
\bm{z}_{n-1}
in (
7.48
)

\displaystyle G(\bm{y}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}d\bm{z}_{n-1}\;\exp\Biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}\bigl(\underline{\bm{z}}_{n-1}-\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}\bigr)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{y_{j}-z_{j}}+e^{z_{j}-y_{j+1}})\Biggr)\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}(\bm{z}_{n-1}).

(7.55)

This integral coincides with the action of
GL
Toda raising operator (
7.18
)

\displaystyle G(\bm{y}_{n})=\bigl[\Lambda_{n}(-\lambda_{n})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}\bigr](\bm{y}_{n}).

(7.56)

By induction assumption the function
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n-1}}(\bm{x}_{n-1})
is continuous and polynomially bounded (
7.54
), hence, due to Corollary
3
the same is true for
G(\bm{y}_{n})
, that is

\displaystyle\bigl|G(\bm{y}_{n})\bigr|\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n-1})\,P(|y_{1}|,\dots,|y_{n}|).

(7.57)

Next we analyse full
BC
eigenfunction (
7.48
). The integrand in the formula (
7.48
)

F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n})}\,\exp\Biggl(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}_{n}-\underline{\bm{y}}_{n}\bigr)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-y_{j+1}})-e^{y_{n}-x_{n}}\Biggr)\\[6.0pt]
\times(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}+g-1}\,\theta(y_{1}-\ln\beta)\;G(\bm{y}_{n})

(7.58)

is smooth in
\bm{x}_{n}
and (by above arguments) continuous in
\bm{y}_{n}
(in the domain
y_{1}>\ln\beta
). Due to (
7.57
) we also have

\bigl|F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})\bigr|\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n})\,\exp\Biggl(-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}+e^{x_{j}-y_{j+1}})-e^{y_{n}-x_{n}}\Biggr)\\[6.0pt]
\times(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{g-1}\,\theta(y_{1}-\ln\beta)\,P(|y_{1}|,\dots,|y_{n}|).

(7.59)

Using this estimate let us prove that for any
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;\Bigl|\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}\,F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})\Bigr|\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n})\,P(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|,|\lambda_{n}|)\,\exp\Biggl(\bigl[k_{1}(\ln\beta-x_{1})-\beta e^{-x_{1}}\bigr]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})\\[6.0pt]
+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\biggl[(k_{j}+k_{j+1})\,\frac{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{j}-x_{j+1}}{2}}\biggr]\,\theta(x_{j}-x_{j+1})\Biggr),

(7.60)

and that this integral converges uniformly in
\bm{x}_{n}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then uniform convergence implies that we can interchange derivatives and integration

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})=\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}\,F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n}),

(7.61)

which gives the stated bound (
7.52
), and from uniform convergence we also infer smoothness of
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
.

To prove the bound (
7.60
) calculate derivatives of
F
(
7.58
). The first order ones are

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})=(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}+e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}-e^{x_{j}-y_{j+1}})F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n}),

(7.62)

where for
j=n
we put
y_{n+1}=\infty
. In general, for
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}^{k}F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})=\sum_{i}p_{i}(\lambda_{n})\,e^{\ell_{i}(y_{j}-x_{j})+m_{i}(x_{j}-y_{j+1})}\,F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})

(7.63)

with polynomials
p_{i}
and integers
0\leq\ell_{i},\,m_{i}\leq k
(in fact,
\ell_{i}+m_{i}\leq k
). Since
F
has factorised form (
7.58
), we can write

\displaystyle\Bigl|\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})\Bigr|\leq\sum_{i}\tilde{p}_{i}(|\lambda_{n}|)\,e^{\ell_{i,1}(y_{1}-x_{1})}\,\prod_{j=2}^{n}e^{\ell_{i,j}(y_{j}-x_{j})+m_{i,j-1}(x_{j-1}-y_{j})}\,\bigl|F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})\bigr|,

(7.64)

where again
\tilde{p}_{i}
are polynomials and
0\leq\ell_{i,j},\,m_{i,j}\leq k_{j}
. Combining the last inequality with (
7.59
) and rearranging some factors we arrive at

\Bigl|\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})\Bigr|\leq C(\bm{\lambda}_{n})\,\sum_{i}\tilde{p}_{i}(|\lambda_{n}|)\,P(|y_{1}|,\dots,|y_{n}|)\\[2.0pt]
\times e^{\ell_{i,1}(y_{1}-x_{1})-e^{y_{1}-x_{1}}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{g-1}\,\theta(y_{1}-\ln\beta)\\[6.0pt]
\times\prod_{j=2}^{n}e^{\ell_{i,j}(y_{j}-x_{j})+m_{i,j-1}(x_{j-1}-y_{j})-e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}-e^{x_{j-1}-y_{j}}}.

(7.65)

Expanding polynomial
P
in terms of monomials
|y_{1}|^{s_{1}}\cdots|y_{n}|^{s_{n}}
we estimate the integral in question

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\bm{y}_{n}\;\Bigl|\partial_{\bm{x}_{n}}^{\bm{k}_{n}}\,F(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n})\Bigr|

(7.66)

by the sum of integrals of the type

\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy_{1}\;|y_{1}|^{s_{1}}\,e^{\ell_{1}(y_{1}-x_{1})-e^{y_{1}-x_{1}}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{g-1}\\[3.0pt]
\times\prod_{j=2}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy_{j}\;|y_{j}|^{s_{j}}\,e^{\ell_{j}(y_{j}-x_{j})+m_{j-1}(x_{j-1}-y_{j})-e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}-e^{x_{j-1}-y_{j}}},

(7.67)

where
s_{j},\ell_{j},m_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\ell_{j},m_{j}\leq k_{j}
. The last multiple integral is factorised, so it is left to bound each of its factors.

Consider the first one-dimensional integral from (
7.67
). After the shift of integration variable
y_{1}=\tilde{y}_{1}+\ln\beta
it can be estimated using Lemma
3

\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy_{1}\;|y_{1}|^{s_{1}}\,e^{\ell_{1}(y_{1}-x_{1})-e^{y_{1}-x_{1}}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{g-1}\\[3.0pt]
\leq P(|x_{1}|)\;e^{[\ell_{1}(\ln\beta-x_{1})-\beta e^{-x_{1}}]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})},

(7.68)

where
P
is polynomial. Furthermore, since
0\leq\ell_{1}\leq k_{1}

\displaystyle e^{\ell_{1}(\ln\beta-x_{1})\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq e^{k_{1}(\ln\beta-x_{1})\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})}.

(7.69)

Next, by Corollary
2
the integrals from the second line of (
7.67
) are bounded as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy_{j}\;|y_{j}|^{s_{j}}\,e^{\ell_{j}(y_{j}-x_{j})+m_{j-1}(x_{j-1}-y_{j})-e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}-e^{x_{j-1}-y_{j}}}\\
\leq P(|x_{j-1}|,|x_{j}|)\;e^{\bigl[(m_{j-1}+\ell_{j})\,\frac{x_{j-1}-x_{j}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{j-1}-x_{j}}{2}}\bigr]\,\theta(x_{j-1}-x_{j})},

(7.70)

where again
P
is polynomial. Since
0\leq\ell_{j},m_{j}\leq k_{j}
, we also have

\displaystyle(m_{j-1}+\ell_{j})\,\frac{x_{j-1}-x_{j}}{2}\,\theta(x_{j-1}-x_{j})\leq(k_{j-1}+k_{j})\,\frac{x_{j-1}-x_{j}}{2}\,\theta(x_{j-1}-x_{j}).

(7.71)

The above inequalities lead to the bound for the integral (
7.67
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy_{1}\;|y_{1}|^{s_{1}}\,e^{\ell_{1}(y_{1}-x_{1})-e^{y_{1}-x_{1}}}\,(1+\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{-g}\,(1-\beta e^{-y_{1}})^{g-1}\\[6.0pt]
&\hskip 42.67912pt\times\prod_{j=2}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy_{j}\;|y_{j}|^{s_{j}}\,e^{\ell_{j}(y_{j}-x_{j})+m_{j-1}(x_{j-1}-y_{j})-e^{y_{j}-x_{j}}-e^{x_{j-1}-y_{j}}}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\leq P(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|)\,\exp\biggl(\bigl[k_{1}(\ln\beta-x_{1})-\beta e^{-x_{1}}\bigr]\,\theta(\ln\beta-x_{1})\\[6.0pt]
&\hskip 128.0374pt+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\biggl[(k_{j-1}+k_{j})\,\frac{x_{j-1}-x_{j}}{2}-e^{\frac{x_{j-1}-x_{j}}{2}}\biggr]\,\theta(x_{j-1}-x_{j})\biggr).\end{aligned}

(7.72)

Together with (
7.65
) this gives the desired estimate (
7.60
). Also notice that both Lemma
3
and Corollary
2
guarantee that all integrals converge uniformly in
x_{j}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Finally, in the case
\bm{k}_{n}=(0,\dots,0)
from recursive formula (
7.48
) we have

\displaystyle\bigl|C^{-1}(\bm{\lambda}_{n})\,\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|\leq C^{-1}(0,\dots,0)\,\Psi_{0,\dots,0}(\bm{x}_{n}).

(7.73)

This proves that the polynomial in the bound (
7.53
) doesn’t depend on
\lambda_{n}
.
∎

7.3
Intertwining relations

Recall the definitions of Toda and DST Lax matrices

\displaystyle L(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}&e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad M(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}&e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x}\partial_{x}&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix},

(7.74)

as well as integral
\mathcal{K}
- and
\mathcal{R}
-operators

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}v}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}v)}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\,\exp\bigl(-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}\bigr)&\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\\
\times&\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1}\,\varphi(-y),\end{aligned}

(7.75)

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}\bigr)\,\varphi(y,x_{1}).

(7.76)

In this section we investigate a natural space of functions, on which all these objects act invariantly and such that the intertwining relations between them hold.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the space of smooth functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
exponentially bounded with all their derivatives, i.e. for every
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
there exist constants
a,b\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|\leq a\,e^{b(|x_{1}|+\ldots+|x_{n}|)}.

(7.77)

Also, for brevity, denote
\mathcal{E}\equiv\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proposition 2
.

The space
\mathcal{E}
has the following properties:

(1)

it is a linear space;

(2)

it is invariant under multiplication by exponents and differentiation

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{E},\quad v\in\mathbb{C},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}\colon\qquad e^{vx}\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E},\qquad\varphi^{(k)}\in\mathcal{E},

(7.78)

and, particularly, the action of Lax matrices elements (
7.74
)

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{E},\quad u\in\mathbb{C}\colon\qquad\bigl(L(u)\bigr)_{rs}\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E},\qquad\bigl(M(u)\bigr)_{rs}\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E};

(7.79)

(3)

it is invariant under action of
\mathcal{K}
-operator (
7.75
)

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{E},\quad\Im v>-g\colon\qquad\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E},

(7.80)

and the corresponding integral
[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi](x)
is uniformly absolutely convergent in
x
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
; furthermore, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the function
\partial_{x}^{k}\,[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi](x)
is analytic in
v
in the domain
\Im v>-g
.

Besides, the space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is invariant under action of
\mathcal{R}
-operator (
7.76
)

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad v\in\mathbb{C}\colon\qquad\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

(7.81)

and the corresponding integral
[\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi](x_{1},x_{2})
is uniformly absolutely convergent in
x_{1},x_{2}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The first two properties of
\mathcal{E}
are simple to check, while the last one follows from Lemma
3
. Namely, for
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
the integrand of
\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)
, see (
7.75
), is smooth in
x,y
and analytic in
v
. Furthermore, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
its derivatives are estimated as

\Bigl|\partial_{x}^{k}\;e^{-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1}\,\varphi(-y)\Bigr|\\[6.0pt]
\leq C\,e^{a(y-\ln\beta)+b|x|+2\Im v\,y-e^{y-x}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{\Im v-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{\Im v+g-1},

(7.82)

where
C,a,b\geq 0
don’t depend on
v
. Consider
v
from any compact subset of complex plane such that

\displaystyle-g<V_{1}\leq\Im v\leq V_{2}.

(7.83)

Then for
y\in(\ln\beta,\infty)
we have inequalities

\displaystyle\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{\Im v+g-1}\leq\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{V_{1}+g-1},

(7.84)

\displaystyle\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{\Im v-g}\leq\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{\Im v-V_{1}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{V_{1}-g}\leq 2^{V_{2}-V_{1}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{V_{1}-g}.

(7.85)

Using them we bound the right hand side of (
7.82
) uniformly in
v
by the function

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\,e^{a^{\prime}(y-\ln\beta)+b|x|-e^{y-x}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-V_{1}-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{V_{1}+g-1}.

(7.86)

Hence, due to Lemma
3
the integral of the left hand side (
7.82
) is exponentially bounded

\displaystyle\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\,\Bigl|\partial_{x}^{k}\,e^{-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1}\,\varphi(-y)\Bigr|\leq C^{\prime\prime}\,e^{b^{\prime}|x|}.

(7.87)

Moreover, Lemma
3
says that the integral from the left converges uniformly in
x
. Thus, we can interchange derivatives and integration

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{k}\,\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi](x)=\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;\partial_{x}^{k}\,e^{-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1}\,\varphi(-y).

(7.88)

Together with the bound (
7.87
) this proves that
\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)
belongs to the space
\mathcal{E}
and converges uniformly in
x
. Moreover, since the bound (
7.86
) is uniform in
v
, the function
\partial_{x}^{k}\,\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi](x)
is analytic in
v
(in the domain
\Im v>-g
).

The proof of the statement about
\mathcal{R}
-operator is analogous, one just uses Corollary
2
instead of Lemma
3
.
∎

The above properties and explicit formulas for
\mathcal{K}
- and
\mathcal{R}
-operators allow us to prove the following intertwining relations.

Proposition 3
.

The matrix relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,M^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M(u-v)\sigma_{2}=M(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathcal{K}(v).

(7.89)

holds on
\mathcal{E}
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
with restriction
\Im v>-g
.

Proposition 4
.

The matrix relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,M_{2}(u-v)=M_{2}(u-v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)

(7.90)

holds on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 6
.

By “matrix relation” we mean four identities corresponding two four matrix entries.

Proof of Proposition
3
.

In Section
2
we show that the reflection equation (
7.89
) is equivalent to three relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,e^{-x}=e^{x}\partial_{x}\,\mathcal{K}(v),

(7.91)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[(\mathrm{i}v+\partial_{x})\,e^{-x}-\beta^{2}\,e^{x}\partial_{x}\bigr]=\bigl[(-\mathrm{i}v-\partial_{x})\,e^{x}\partial_{x}+2\alpha+\beta^{2}\,e^{-x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v),

(7.92)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(v)\,\bigl[(v-\mathrm{i}\partial_{x})^{2}+2\alpha\,e^{x}\partial_{x}+\beta^{2}\,(e^{x}\partial_{x})^{2}\bigr]=\bigl[(v-\mathrm{i}\partial_{x})^{2}+2\alpha\,e^{-x}+\beta^{2}\,e^{-2x}\bigr]\,\mathcal{K}(v),

(7.93)

which formally hold due to explicit formula for the kernel of reflection operator (
7.75
)

\displaystyle K(x,y)=\frac{(2\beta)^{\mathrm{i}v}}{\Gamma(g-\mathrm{i}v)}\,e^{-2\mathrm{i}vy-e^{y-x}}\,\bigl(1+\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v-g}\,\bigl(1-\beta e^{-y}\bigr)^{-\mathrm{i}v+g-1},

(7.94)

where
g=1/2+\alpha/\beta
.
It remains to justify several steps:

(1)

all integrals on the way converge;

(2)

derivatives
\partial_{x},\partial_{x}^{2}
can be interchanged with integral over
y
;

(3)

boundary terms from integrating by parts vanish.

First, notice that by Proposition
2
all above relations are well defined on
\mathcal{E}
. To prove the first one (
7.91
) we need to interchange derivative
\partial_{x}
with integral over
y
from the right

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;K(x,y)\,\varphi(-y)=\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;\partial_{x}\,K(x,y)\,\varphi(-y)=e^{-x}\,\int_{\ln\beta}^{\infty}dy\;K(x,y)\,e^{y}\,\varphi(-y),

(7.95)

where
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}
. By Proposition
2
the function
\tilde{\varphi}(y)=e^{-y}\varphi(y)
belongs to
\mathcal{E}
. Consequently, by the same proposition the integral
\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\tilde{\varphi}\bigr](x)
converges uniformly in
x
, which allows us to switch order of derivative
\partial_{x}
and integration. The same argument can be applied to the right hand sides of the remaining two relations (
7.92
), (
7.93
).

Finally, to have vanishing boundary terms after integrating by parts (up to two times) in the left hand sides we assume
\Im v>2-g
, so that

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\ln\beta^{+}}K(x,y)=\lim_{y\to\ln\beta^{+}}\partial_{y}K(x,y)=0,

(7.96)

see (
7.94
). This justifies the relations (
7.92
), (
7.93
). To weaken the assumption on
v
recall that by Proposition
2
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the function
\partial_{x}^{k}\,\bigl[\mathcal{K}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x)
is analytic in
v
under restriction
\Im v>-g
. Hence, once the above relations are proved assuming
\Im v>2-g
, they can be analytically continued.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

The matrix relation in question is equivalent to the relations

\displaystyle e^{\pm x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,e^{\pm x_{2}},

(7.97)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,(\mathrm{i}v-e^{x_{2}-x_{1}}+\partial_{x_{2}}),

(7.98)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{2}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)=e^{-x_{2}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,e^{x_{1}},

(7.99)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\partial_{x_{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,(\mathrm{i}v-e^{x_{2}-x_{1}})+e^{-x_{2}}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,e^{x_{1}},

(7.100)

which again hold formally due to explicit formula for the kernel of
\mathcal{R}
-operator (
7.76
). The rest of the proof uses Proposition
2
and is absolutely analogous to the previous one.
∎

In
BC
Toda chain we also encounter modified Lax matrices

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(u)=\sigma_{2}\,L^{t}(-u)\,\sigma_{2},\qquad\widetilde{M}(u)=\sigma_{2}\,M^{t}(-u)\,\sigma_{2}

(7.101)

and
\mathcal{R}
-operator with reflected coordinate

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)=r_{1}\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,r_{1},\qquad[r_{1}\varphi](x_{1},x_{2})=\varphi(-x_{1},x_{2}).

(7.102)

Using transpositions, reflections and multiplying by
\sigma_{2}
one can easily obtain the following relations from (
7.90
).

Corollary 4
.

The matrix relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\widetilde{M}_{2}(u+v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)=\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\,\widetilde{M}_{2}(u+v)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v),

(7.103)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,M_{2}^{t}(-u-v)\,L_{1}(u)=L_{1}(u)\,M_{2}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v),

(7.104)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\,\widetilde{M}_{2}^{t}(v-u)=\widetilde{M}_{2}^{t}(v-u)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)

(7.105)

hold on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 7
.

Since the bound (
7.77
) is factorised in
x_{j}
, it is straightforward to show that in all statements above one can replace
\mathcal{E}\equiv\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})
or
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
k\geq 2
, assuming that
\mathcal{K}
- and
\mathcal{R}
-operators act on extra variables as identity operators. For example, for every
j\in\{1,...,k\}
the operator
\mathcal{K}_{j}(v)
acts invariantly on the space of functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{k})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
(where the index of reflection operator indicates on which variable it acts nontrivially).

Proposition
2
and the last remark imply commutativity of operators with different indices.

Lemma 5
.

The operators
L_{1}(u_{1})
,
M_{2}(u_{2})
,
\mathcal{K}_{3}(u_{3})
and
\mathcal{R}_{45}(u_{4})
mutually commute on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{5})

for all
u_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
with restriction
\Im u_{3}>-g
.

Proof.

Entries of
L_{1}
and
M_{2}
consist of exponents
e^{\pm x_{1}},e^{\pm x_{2}}
and derivatives
\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}
. They commute with each other since functions from
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{5})
are smooth.

Commutativity of
\mathcal{K}
- and
\mathcal{R}
-operators with Lax matrices follows from their commutativity with derivatives
\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}}
, which is guaranteed by the invariance of the space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{5})
under differentiation. Besides, the actions of operators
\mathcal{K}_{3}
and
\mathcal{R}_{45}
commute thanks to absolute convergence of corresponding integrals.
∎

7.4
Monodromy matrices and operators

Monodromy matrix and monodromy operator for
GL
system are defined as

\displaystyle T_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\cdots L_{1}(u),\qquad U_{na}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v).

(7.106)

Due to Proposition
2
and Remark
7
the space of functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n},x_{a})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
is invariant under the action of monodromy matrix and monodromy operator. Inductive usage of Proposition
4
and Lemma
5
gives the following statement.

Proposition 5
.

The matrix relation

\displaystyle U_{na}(v)\,T_{n}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)=M_{a}(u-v)\,T_{n}(u)\,U_{na}(v)

(7.107)

holds on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction over
n
. The case
n=1
coincides with Proposition
4
. Consider the induction step
n-1\to n
and assume we proved the identity

\displaystyle U_{n-1,a}(v)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)=M_{a}(u-v)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,U_{n-1,a}(v).

(7.108)

By definition,

\displaystyle U_{na}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,U_{n-1,a}(v),\qquad T_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\,T_{n-1}(u),

(7.109)

so let us multiply (
7.108
) by
\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)
. The result of multiplication is well defined on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, since this space is invariant under action of all operators.

Due to Lemma
5
the operators
L_{n}
and
U_{n-1,a}
commute on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, so from the left we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,U_{n-1,a}(v)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)\\[6.0pt]
&=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,U_{n-1,a}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)=U_{na}(v)\,T_{n}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v),\end{aligned}

(7.110)

as desired (
7.107
). From the right we can use Proposition
4

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,U_{n-1,a}(v)\\[6.0pt]
&=M_{a}(u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,U_{n-1,a}(v),\end{aligned}

(7.111)

since for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
the functions
\bigl(T_{n-1}(u)\bigr)_{rs}\,U_{n-1,a}(v)\,\varphi
are also from
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. It is left to again invoke Lemma
5
to interchange operators
\mathcal{R}_{na}
and
T_{n-1}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &M_{a}(u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,U_{n-1,a}(v)\\[6.0pt]
&=M_{a}(u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\,T_{n-1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,U_{n-1,a}(v)=M_{a}(u-v)\,T_{n}(u)\,U_{na}(v),\end{aligned}

(7.112)

where the last expression coincides with the right hand side of the statement (
1.30
).
∎

Now recall definitions of monodromy matrix and monodromy operator for
BC
system

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\cdots L_{1}(u)\,K(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{1}(u)\cdots\widetilde{L}_{n}(u),

(7.113)

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)\,\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1a}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v).

(7.114)

By Proposition
2
these operators act invariantly on the space of functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n},x_{a})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. Besides, Propositions
3
,
4
together with Corollary
4
and Lemma
5
imply the following relation.

Proposition 6
.

The matrix relation

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)=M_{a}(u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}_{a}(u+v)\;\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)

(7.115)

holds on
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
under restriction
\Im v>-g
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction over
n
. The base case
n=0
coincides with Proposition
3
. Consider the induction step
n-1\to n
. By definition,

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v),\qquad\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)=L_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u).

(7.116)

Inserting this into the left hand side of (
7.115
) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\\[6.0pt]
&=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\Bigr]\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u).\end{aligned}

(7.117)

The square brackets emphasize the part, where one can use the relation with
\mathcal{R}^{*}
-operator (
7.104
). That is,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\Bigr]\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\\[6.0pt]
&=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\Bigl[L_{n}(u)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\Bigr]\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\\[6.0pt]
&=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\Bigr],\end{aligned}

(7.118)

where passing to the last line we use the fact that
\mathcal{R}^{*}
-operator commutes with all matrices inside
\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)
(Lemma
5
). Again, in square brackets in the last line one can use relation with
\mathcal{R}^{*}
-operator (
7.105
). Besides, note that the operator
\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)
comutes with
L_{n}(u)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\Bigl[\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\Bigr]\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\Bigr]\\[6.0pt]
&=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\Bigl[\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\Bigr]\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v).\end{aligned}

(7.119)

In the last expression the product in square brackets resembles the left hand side of the claimed relation (
7.115
) on the previous step of induction. Using induction assumption we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\Bigl[\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{M}^{t}_{a}(v-u)\Bigr]\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\\[6.0pt]
&=\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\Bigl[M_{a}(u-v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{M}_{a}(u+v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\Bigr]\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v).\end{aligned}

(7.120)

Now it is left to move the first
\mathcal{R}
-operator through all the matrices with the help of relations (
7.90
), (
7.103
) and Lemma
5
. Namely,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigl[\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,L_{n}(u)\,M_{a}(u-v)\Bigr]\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{M}_{a}(u+v)\,\Bigr[\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\Bigr]\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\\[6.0pt]
&=M_{a}(u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\Bigr]\,\widetilde{M}_{a}(u+v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\\[6.0pt]
&=M_{a}(u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\Bigl[\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\widetilde{M}_{a}(u+v)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\Bigr]\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\\[6.0pt]
&=M_{a}(u-v)\,L_{n}(u)\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\widetilde{L}_{n}(u)\,\widetilde{M}_{a}(u+v)\,\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\,\mathbb{U}_{n-1,a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v).\end{aligned}

(7.121)

The last expression coincides with the right hand side of the claimed relation (
7.115
).
∎

7.5
Baxter operators

Baxter operators for
GL
and
BC
Toda systems are defined as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &Q_{n}(v)=\lim_{x_{a}\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})},\\[6.0pt]
&\mathbb{Q}_{n}(v)=\lim_{x_{a}\to\infty}\mathcal{R}_{na}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)\,\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1a}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})},\end{aligned}

(7.122)

where the action of operator products is restricted to the functions of the variables
\bm{x}_{n}
. These operators are not well defined on the whole space of exponentially tempered functions
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which we studied in previous sections. However, one can pick up a suitable subspace.

Fix
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the space of smooth functions
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for every
\bm{k}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
there exist constants
a,b\geq 0
and polynomial
P(x_{j})
satisfying

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\bigr|\leq ae^{b(|x_{1}|+\ldots+|x_{j-1}|+|x_{j+1}|+\ldots+|x_{n}|)}\times\left\{\begin{aligned} &\,P(x_{j}),&&\;x_{j}\geq 0,\\[6.0pt]
&\,e^{b|x_{j}|},&&\;x_{j}\leq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

(7.123)

Clearly, it is a subspace of exponentially tempered functions
\mathcal{E}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Due to definition of Baxter operators (
7.122
) it is natural to introduce the operator
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)
that acts on functions
\varphi(x_{1},x_{2})
by the formula

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}\bigr)\,\varphi(y,x_{1}).

(7.124)

Surely, it represents the limit of previously studied
\mathcal{R}
-operator

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}\bigr)\,\varphi(y,x_{1})

(7.125)

as
x_{2}\to\infty
, but as always one needs to make sure that the limit and integration can be interchanged. Further, it is not hard to see that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-operator
is well defined on
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
under assumption
\Im v<0
. As opposed to
\mathcal{R}
-operator, it is not well defined on the larger space
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Proposition 7
.

(1)

The space
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is linear, invariant under differentiation and multiplication by exponents

\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\Re v\leq 0\colon\qquad e^{vx}\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}).

(7.126)

(2)

Let
\Im v<0
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then
\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1})\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})
, the corresponding integral is uniformly absolutely convergent in
x_{1}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
, and the following limits hold true

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{2}\to\infty}\,\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1}),

(7.127)

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{2}\to\infty}\,\partial_{x_{2}}\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=0.

(7.128)

(3)

Let
\Im v<0
and
\varphi(x_{1})\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and the corresponding integral is uniformly absolutely convergent in
x_{1},x_{2}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The first item is simple to check, so consider the second one. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
derivatives of the integrand (
7.124
) are bounded as follows

\displaystyle\Bigl|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\Bigr|\leq\sum_{j}a_{j}\,e^{b_{j}|x_{1}|+c_{j}(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\times\left\{\begin{aligned} &\,P(y),&&\;y\geq 0,\\[6.0pt]
&\,e^{d|y|},&&\;y\leq 0,\end{aligned}\right.

(7.129)

where
P
is polynomial,
a_{j},b_{j},d\geq 0
and
c_{j}>0
(since
\Im v<0
). From this let us prove that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;\Bigl|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\Bigr|\leq A\,e^{B|x_{1}|}

(7.130)

for some
A,B\geq 0
and that convergence of the above integral is uniform in
x_{1}
from compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
. Uniformity allows to interchange derivatives and integration in the expression

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1}}^{k}\,\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1})=\partial_{x_{1}}^{k}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1}),

(7.131)

which in turn implies smoothness of
\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1})
, while the specific bound (
7.130
) shows that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Divide the integral in (
7.130
) into two parts

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy=\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy+\int_{0}^{\infty}dy

(7.132)

and consider the first one. By (
7.129
) it is sufficient to prove the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\;e^{-(c+d)y-e^{x_{1}-y}}\leq A\,e^{B|x_{1}|}.

(7.133)

Using the fact that
e^{x_{1}-y}\geq e^{-|x_{1}|-y}
and changing integration variable
z=-|x_{1}|-y
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\;e^{-(c+d)y-e^{x_{1}-y}}&\leq e^{(c+d)|x_{1}|}\int_{-|x_{1}|}^{\infty}dz\;e^{(c+d)z-e^{z}}\\[6.0pt]
&\leq e^{(c+d)|x_{1}|}\int_{-|x_{1}|}^{0}dz\;e^{(c+d)z}+e^{(c+d)|x_{1}|}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{(c+d)z-e^{z}}.\end{aligned}

(7.134)

The last sum of integrals is clearly bounded in the needed way.

Now consider the second integral in (
7.132
). Due to (
7.129
) it is sufficient to prove the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;P(y)\,e^{-cy-e^{x_{1}-y}}\leq A

(7.135)

where
c>0
. The latter is obvious since
e^{-e^{x_{1}-y}}\leq 1
. This concludes the proof of inequality (
7.130
). All above estimates of integrands are clearly uniform in
x_{1}
from compact sets.

Next let us prove the equality (
7.127
), which states that

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{2}\to\infty}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})

(7.136)

assuming
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\Im v<0
.
To interchange limit and integration we use dominated convergence theorem. Namely, notice that

\displaystyle\Bigl|e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\Bigr|\leq\Bigl|e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\Bigr|.

(7.137)

Function from the right is integrable, since it represents the integrand of
\bigl[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1})
.

Finally, let us prove the equality (
7.128
). Explicitly, it reads

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{2}\to\infty}\,\partial_{x_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})=0.

(7.138)

Again, divide this integral into two parts with integration over
y\leq 0
and
y\geq 0
. For
y\leq 0
derivative of integrand is bounded as

\displaystyle\Bigl|\partial_{x_{2}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\Bigr|\leq a\,e^{-x_{2}+b|x_{1}|+c|y|-e^{x_{1}-y}}

(7.139)

with some constants
a,b,c
. The right hand side is bounded uniformly in
x_{2}\geq 0
, hence we are allowed to interchange the derivative and integration

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})=\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\;\partial_{x_{2}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1}).

(7.140)

Moreover, due to the bound (
7.139
)

\displaystyle\biggl|\partial_{x_{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\biggr|\leq a\,e^{-x_{2}+b|x_{1}|}\,\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\;e^{c|y|-e^{x_{1}-y}}\;\to\;0

(7.141)

as
x_{2}\to\infty
(since the last integral converges). This proves
y\leq 0
part of the equality (
7.138
).

In the remaining
y\geq 0
part we have the bound

\displaystyle\Bigl|\partial_{x_{2}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\Bigr|\leq P(y)\,e^{b|x_{1}|+(\Im v+1)y-x_{2}-e^{y-x_{2}}},

(7.142)

where
P
is polynomial. The right hand side is bounded uniformly in
x_{2}\geq 0
, so that again

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})=\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;\partial_{x_{2}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1}).

(7.143)

Next split the last integral into two parts

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dy=\int_{0}^{x_{2}}dy+\int_{x_{2}}^{\infty}dy,

(7.144)

where we may assume
x_{2}>0
since we are interested in the limit
x_{2}\to\infty
. It is easy to prove that the first part tends to zero using (
7.142
). Namely,

\biggl|\int_{0}^{x_{2}}dy\;\partial_{x_{2}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\biggr|\\[3.0pt]
\leq e^{b|x_{1}|-x_{2}}\,\int_{0}^{x_{2}}dy\;P(y)\,e^{(\Im v+1)y}\leq e^{b|x_{1}|-x_{2}}\,P(x_{2})\,\frac{e^{(\Im v+1)x_{2}}-1}{\Im v+1}\;\to\;0

(7.145)

as
x_{2}\to\infty
since
\Im v<0
. In the second part we again use (
7.142
) and change the integration variable
z=y-x_{2}
, so that

\displaystyle\biggl|\int_{x_{2}}^{\infty}dy\;\partial_{x_{2}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}-e^{y-x_{2}}}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})\biggr|\leq e^{b|x_{1}|+\Im v\,x_{2}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;P(z+x_{2})\,e^{(\Im v+1)z-e^{z}}.

(7.146)

Writing polynomial
P
in terms of monomials
z^{\ell_{j}}x_{2}^{m_{j}}
we see again that the last expression tends to zero as
x_{2}\to\infty
. This concludes the proof of equality (
7.138
) and of the second item of this proposition.

The proof of the third item of this proposition is absolutely analogous.
∎

By Propositions
2
,
7
and Remark
7
Baxter operators (
7.122
) are well defined on the space
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The following corollary is used in Section
4
.

Corollary 5
.

Let
\Im v\in(-g,0)
and
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(v)\,\varphi=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{na}(v)\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,a}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)\,\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1a}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(v)\,\varphi

(7.147)

and
\mathbb{Q}_{n}(v)\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Besides, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{a}\to\infty}\,\partial_{x_{a}}\bigl[\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](\bm{x}_{n},x_{a})=0.

(7.148)

Remark 8
.

Baxter operators for
GL
Toda system can be rewritten in a similar way

\displaystyle Q_{n}(v)\,\varphi=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{na}(v)\,\mathcal{R}_{n-1,a}(v)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(v)\,\varphi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(7.149)

acting on
\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and assuming
\Im v<0
.

By definition the generating function of
BC
Toda Hamiltonians equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}}&e^{-x_{n}}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathbb{T}_{n-1}(u)\,\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x_{n}}\\[3.0pt]
-u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{n}}\end{pmatrix},

(7.150)

which means that it doesn’t contain exponents
e^{x_{n}}
(as opposed to other elements of
\mathbb{T}_{n}(u)
). Then due to Propositions
2
,
7
we have the following statement, which is also used in Section
4
.

Corollary 6
.

The operator
\mathbb{B}_{n}(u)
acts invariantly on the space
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

At last, let us remark that the actions of monodromy and Baxter operators on
BC
Toda eigenfunctions
\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
are well defined due to Proposition
1
and the above statements. Namely, since the function

\displaystyle\exp\bigl([kx-e^{x}]\,\theta(x)\bigr)\leq C(k)

(7.151)

is bounded uniformly in
x
, from Proposition
1
we deduce the following corollary.

Corollary 7
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\Psi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})\;\in\;\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\;\subset\;\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(7.152)

Remark 9
.

Using Corollary
2
one can prove the same statement for
GL
Toda eigenfunctions, that is
\Phi_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{E}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
\bm{\lambda}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Appendix A
Commutativity of Baxter operators

In the second part of this work
[
BDK
]
we prove commutativity of Baxter operators

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\rho)=\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\rho)\,\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)

(A.1)

using diagram technique. However, there is another approach based on Yang–Baxter and reflection equations, which has been used in the recent works on spin chains
[
ADV1
,
ADV2
]
. Below we briefly describe (omitting details) how it works for both
GL
and
BC
Toda chains.

A.1
GL
Toda chain

By definition (
1.44
), the Baxter operator is the degeneration of the monodromy operator

\displaystyle Q_{n}(\lambda)=\lim_{x_{a}\to\infty}U_{na}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})},\qquad U_{na}(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(\lambda).

(A.2)

The latter is constructed from integral operators
\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)
intertwining Toda and DST Lax matrices

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,L_{1}(u)\,M_{2}(u-v)=M_{2}(u-v)\,L_{1}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v).

(A.3)

As shown in
[
KSS
]
, there also exists operator that intertwines two DST Lax matrices

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(v)\,M_{1}(u)\,M_{2}(u-v)=M_{2}(u-v)\,M_{1}(u)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(v).

(A.4)

Explicitly, its action on the function
\varphi(x_{1},x_{2})
is given by

\displaystyle\bigl[\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{-\infty}^{x_{2}}dy\;\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(x_{1}-y)-e^{x_{1}-y}+e^{x_{1}-x_{2}}\bigr)\,\bigl(1-e^{y-x_{2}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{i}v-1}\,\varphi(y,x_{1})

(A.5)

where singularity at
y=x_{2}
is integrable if
\Re(\mathrm{i}v)>0
.

Now consider the permutation of Lax matrices

\displaystyle L_{1}(w)\,M_{2}(w-v)\,M_{3}(w-u)\quad\longrightarrow\quad M_{3}(w-u)\,M_{2}(w-v)\,L_{1}(w).

(A.6)

It can be realized in two distinct ways using the above
\mathcal{R}
-operators, which suggests the following Yang–Baxter relation between them

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{23}(u-v)\,\mathcal{R}_{13}(u)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\mathcal{R}_{13}(u)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{23}(u-v).

(A.7)

Its proof is straightforward and boils down to the
star-triangle identity
from our subsequent paper
[
BDK
]
. By standard arguments, iterating the last formula many times we obtain the relation with monodromy operators

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}(u-v)\,U_{nb}(u)\,U_{na}(v)=U_{na}(v)\,U_{nb}(u)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}(u-v).

(A.8)

Finally, acting from both sides on the function independent of
x_{a},x_{b}
and taking the limits
x_{a},x_{b}\to\infty
we obtain the commutativity of
GL
Baxter operators

\displaystyle Q_{n}(u)\,Q_{n}(v)=Q_{n}(v)\,Q_{n}(u).

(A.9)

A.2
BC
Toda chain

By definition (
4.1
), the Baxter operator is the degeneration of the monodromy operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\lambda)=\lim_{x_{a}\to\infty}\mathbb{U}_{na}(\lambda)\bigr|_{\varphi(\bm{x}_{n})},\qquad\mathbb{U}_{na}(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}_{na}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}_{1a}(\lambda)\,\mathcal{K}_{a}(\lambda)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{1a}(\lambda)\cdots\mathcal{R}^{*}_{na}(\lambda).

(A.10)

The latter consists of integral operators
\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)
intertwining Toda and DST Lax matrices (
A.3
), their reflected versions
\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)
intertwining Toda and transposed DST Lax matrices

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v)\,M^{t}_{2}(-u-v)\,L_{1}(u)=L_{1}(u)\,M^{t}_{2}(-u-v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{12}(v),

(A.11)

and the operator
\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)
satisfying reflection equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{a}(v)\,M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}(u-v)\sigma_{2}=M_{a}(u-v)\,K(u)\,\sigma_{2}M_{a}^{t}(-u-v)\sigma_{2}\;\mathcal{K}_{a}(v).

(A.12)

To prove commutativity of Baxter operators we need some relation with two monodromy operators, similar to (
A.8
).

For this we consider the operator that intertwines DST and transposed DST Lax matrices

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(v)\,M_{2}^{t}(-u)\,M_{1}(u-v)=M_{1}(u-v)\,M_{2}^{t}(-u)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(v).

(A.13)

This equation can be solved analogously to the one for
\mathcal{K}
-operator (see Section
2
), so that at the end one finds

\bigl[\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(v)\,\varphi\bigr](x_{1},x_{2})=\int{R}dy_{2}\,\int_{y_{2}}^{\infty}dy_{1}\,\exp\bigl(\mathrm{i}v(y_{2}-y_{1})-e^{-x_{1}-y_{2}}-e^{y_{1}-x_{2}}\bigr)\\[2.0pt]
\times(1-e^{y_{2}-y_{1}})^{-\mathrm{i}v-1}\,\varphi(-y_{1},y_{2}).

(A.14)

Now consider the permutation of matrices

M_{2}^{t}(-w-u)\,M_{1}^{t}(-w-v)\,K(w)\,\sigma_{2}M_{1}(w-v)\,M_{2}(w-u)\sigma_{2}\\[6.0pt]
\longrightarrow\quad M_{2}(w-u)\,M_{1}(w-v)\,K(w)\,\sigma_{2}M_{1}^{t}(-w-v)\,M_{2}^{t}(-w-u)\sigma_{2}.

(A.15)

It can be accomplished in two different ways using two DST intertwiners (
A.4
), (
A.13
) and reflection operator (
A.12
), which suggests the following reflection equation between them

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(u-v)\,\mathcal{K}_{2}(u)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(u+v)\,\mathcal{K}_{1}(v)=\mathcal{K}_{1}(v)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(u+v)\,\mathcal{K}_{2}(u)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{12}(u-v).

(A.16)

Its proof is straightforward and boils down to the integral identity which we call
flip relation
in our subsequent paper
[
BDK
]
.

Besides, using explicit formulas for
\mathcal{R}
-operators one can prove another Yang–Baxter-type equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}_{13}(u)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{23}(u+v)\,\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)=\mathcal{R}_{12}(v)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{23}(u+v)\,\mathcal{R}^{*}_{13}(u).

(A.17)

This identity can be also guessed from permutation of Toda and DST Lax matrices.

Hence, in a standard way
[
Skl2
]
one can combine relations (
A.7
), (
A.16
) and (
A.17
) to derive reflection equation for the monodromy operator (
A.10
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}(u-v)\,\mathbb{U}_{nb}(u)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}(u+v)\,\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)=\mathbb{U}_{na}(v)\,\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}(u+v)\,\mathbb{U}_{nb}(u)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}(u-v).

(A.18)

At last, acting on a function independent of
x_{a},x_{b}
and taking the limits
x_{a},x_{b}\to\infty
one arrives (after some manipulations with integrals) at the commutativity of Baxter operators (
A.10
).

Appendix B
Relation to XXX spin chain

In this section we systematically itemize various

s\ell_{2}
-invariant solutions of the Yang–Baxter and reflection equations.

The simplest solution of the Yang–Baxter equation is the
Yang’s
R
-matrix. On the next level one obtains a more complicated
solution — the
\mathsf{L}
-operator associated with the XXX spin chain.
The Yang–Baxter equation on this level is the Yangian algebra
relation involving the Yang’s
R
-matrix and
\mathsf{L}
-operators.
Using various reductions of the
\mathsf{L}
-operator we
obtain
L^{\pm}
-operators, which are other solutions of
the Yangian algebra relation involving the Yang’s
R
-matrix.
We then show that the
M
-operator (
1.25
)
essentially coincides with the
L^{+}
-operator written in a different representation.
Finally, using an appropriate reduction of the
M
-operator we
obtain the Toda
L
-operator (
1.16
) and show that it
is a solution of the same Yangian algebra relation
involving the Yang’s
R
-matrix.
Thus we show that various solutions of the Yangian algebra relation
involving the Yang’s
R
-matrix can be obtained from the
\mathsf{L}
-operator.

On the next level one obtains the Yang–Baxter relation involving

\mathsf{L}
-operators and the general
\mathsf{R}
-operator that appears in the study of the XXX spin chain.
The
\mathsf{R}
-operator is realized as an integral operator acting on
functions of two variables.
Then, in full analogy with the previous level, we show that
the operators
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}

from the previous section, needed for the proof of the commutativity
of the
Q
-operators, and the operators
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{R}^{*}

needed for the construction of the eigenfunctions can be obtained
by appropriate reductions of the
\mathsf{R}
-operator.

Next we turn to the reflection equation.
The general
s\ell_{2}
-invariant solution of the reflection equation —
the integral
\mathsf{K}
-operator for the XXX spin chain — is obtained in
[
FGK
,
ABDK
]
.
Using appropriate reductions we derive from the integral
\mathsf{K}
-operator
the representation (
1.65
) for the reflection operator
\mathcal{K}
.
This provides a second independent derivation and an additional cross-check
of the representation (
1.65
).

It will be interesting to see whether the considered reflection operators can be obtained from some universal
K
-operator, see
[
AV
]
and references therein.

Remark 10
.

As opposed to the previous sections, many arguments in this part are made on the physical level of rigour. It is an open problem to rigorously analyse the most general
\mathsf{R}
- and
\mathsf{K}
-operators (associated with the XXX spin chain), see
[
N
]
for some recent progress in this direction.

B.1
Yang–Baxter equation

The
R
-operator is the general solution of the Yang–Baxter equation with symmetry algebra
s\ell_{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{12}(u-v)\,\mathsf{R}_{13}(u)\,\mathsf{R}_{23}(v)=\mathsf{R}_{23}(v)\,\mathsf{R}_{13}(u)\,\mathsf{R}_{12}(u-v)\,.

(B.1)

In (
B.1
) we have the operator relation in the tensor product of three representations
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\otimes V_{3}
, each operator
\mathsf{R}_{ij}
acts nontrivially in spaces
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
and as the identity operator in the third space.

In the simplest case of two-dimensional representations
V_{1}=V_{2}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
the operator
\mathsf{R}_{12}(u)
degenerates into the finite-dimensional solution of Yang–Baxter equation — the Yang’s
R
-matrix acting
in the tensor product
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}

\mathsf{R}_{12}(u)=u+P\,,\qquad P\,a\otimes b=b\otimes a\,.

B.1.1
Yangian algebra

The more complicated solution of Yang–Baxter
relation (
B.1
) — the
\mathsf{L}
-operator for the XXX spin chain — appears in the case when
V_{1}=V_{2}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
V_{3}
is the space of arbitrary representation of the algebra

s\ell_{2}
with generators
S^{+}\,,S^{-}
and
S
.
In this case
\mathsf{R}_{12}(u-v)
is given by the Yang’s
R
-matrix
and the general
R
-operators
\mathsf{R}_{13}(u)
and
\mathsf{R}_{23}(v)
are reduced
up to the shift of spectral parameters to the
\mathsf{L}
-operators

\displaystyle\textstyle\left(u-v+P\right)\,\bigl(\mathsf{L}(u+\frac{1}{2})\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathsf{L}(v+\frac{1}{2})\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathsf{L}(v+\frac{1}{2})\bigr)\,\bigl(\mathsf{L}(u+\frac{1}{2})\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\left(u-v+P\right)\,,

where the explicit expression for the
\mathsf{L}
-operator reads

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(u)=u\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}S&S_{-}\\
S_{+}&-S\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+S&S_{-}\\
S_{+}&u-S\end{array}\right).

The
\mathsf{L}
-operator has linear dependence on spectral parameter
u

and the coefficient in front of
u
is unit matrix.
Note that after the shifts
u\to u-\frac{1}{2}
and
v\to v-\frac{1}{2}

one obtains the previous relation in a standard form

\displaystyle\textstyle\left(u-v+P\right)\,\bigl(\mathsf{L}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathsf{L}(v)\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathsf{L}(v)\bigr)\,\bigl(\mathsf{L}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\left(u-v+P\right)\,.

(B.8)

The
s\ell_{2}
Lie algebra generators appear in
the evaluation representations of the Yangian algebra, which is
generated by the matrix elements of the
\mathsf{L}
-operator with
the fundamental relation (
B.8
) being the
algebra relations

[S_{+}\,,S_{-}]=2S
,
[S\,,S_{\pm}]=\pm S_{\pm}
.
Apart from the standard representations considered above, there
are other Yangian representations, described by

L^{\pm}
-operators obeying (
B.8
) but different from
(
B.1.1
). These
L^{\pm}
-operators can be obtained from the ordinary

\mathsf{L}
-operators in the appropriate limits.
The
L^{\pm}
-operators have linear dependence on spectral parameter
u

but the coefficients in front of
u
are one-dimensional projectors.

We present the necessary calculations for completing the picture.
The operators
L^{\pm}
appear in the dimer self-trapping (DST)
chain model
[
KSS
,
Skl3
]
.
Let us realize
s\ell_{2}
generators in a standard way

\displaystyle S=z\partial_{z}+s\,,\qquad S_{-}=-\partial_{z}\,,\qquad S_{+}=z^{2}\partial_{z}+2sz\,.

(B.9)

In this representation the
\mathsf{L}
-operator can be factorized in two ways

\mathsf{L}(u)=\mathsf{L}(u_{1},u_{2})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+s+z\partial&-\partial\\
z^{2}\partial+2sz&u-s-z\partial\end{array}\right)\\
=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
z&u_{2}\end{array}\right)L^{+}(u_{1})=L^{-}(u_{2})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u_{1}&0\\
-z&1\end{array}\right),

(B.10)

where
u_{1}=u+s-1\,,u_{2}=u-s
and

\displaystyle L^{+}(u)=u\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial z&-\partial\\
-z&1\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+\partial z&-\partial\\
-z&1\end{array}\right)\ ;

\displaystyle L^{-}(u)=u\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-\partial\\
z&-z\partial\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-\partial\\
z&u-z\partial\end{array}\right).

Note relation

\displaystyle L^{+}(u)L^{-}(v)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+\partial z&-\partial\\
-z&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-\partial\\
z&v-z\partial\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u&-(u+v)\partial\\
0&v\end{array}\right)

and as consequence

\displaystyle L^{+}(u)L^{-}(-u)=u\sigma_{3}\ \ \ ;\ \ \ \sigma_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)\,.

Using factorization it is easy to derive the leading asymptotics of
two-parametric
\mathsf{L}
-operator in the limit when one
parameter is fixed and second goes to infinity:

\mathsf{L}(u_{1},u_{2})\xrightarrow{u_{1}\to\infty}L^{-}(u_{2})\,\Lambda_{-}(u_{1})\ \ ;\ \ \mathsf{L}(u_{1},u_{2})\xrightarrow{u_{2}\to\infty}\Lambda_{+}(u_{2})\,L^{+}(u_{1}),

(B.31)

where

\displaystyle\Lambda_{-}(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\ \ ;\ \ \Lambda_{+}(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&u\end{array}\right).

To prove that the operators
L^{\pm}(u)
satisfy the Yangian relation
(
B.8
) we rewrite the previous formulae using the shift

u\to u+s
in the case of
L^{-}
and
u\to u-s
in the
case of
L^{+}

\mathsf{L}(u+s)\xrightarrow{s\to\infty}L^{-}(u)\,\Lambda_{-}(2s)\ \ ;\ \ \mathsf{L}(u-s)\xrightarrow{s\to\infty}\Lambda_{+}(2s)\,\sigma_{3}\,L^{+}(u-1).

(B.36)

Using the shifts
u\to u+s
and
v\to v+s
in Yangian relation
(
B.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle\left(u-v+P\right)\,\bigl(\mathsf{L}(u+s)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathsf{L}(v+s)\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes\mathsf{L}(v+s)\bigr)\,\bigl(\mathsf{L}(u+s)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\left(u-v+P\right).

Taking the asymptotics
s\to\infty
gives

\displaystyle\textstyle\left(u-v+P\right)\,\bigl(L^{-}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes L^{-}(v)\bigr)\,\Lambda_{-}(2s)\otimes\Lambda_{-}(2s)=

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes L^{-}(v)\bigr)\,\bigl(L^{-}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\Lambda_{-}(2s)\otimes\Lambda_{-}(2s)\,\left(u-v+P\right).

Furthermore, using
s\ell_{2}
invariance of the Yang’s
R
-matrix

\displaystyle\Lambda_{-}(2s)\otimes\Lambda_{-}(2s)\,\left(u-v+P\right)=\left(u-v+P\right)\,\Lambda_{-}(2s)\otimes\Lambda_{-}(2s)

(B.37)

we derive

\displaystyle\textstyle\left(u-v+P\right)\,\bigl(L^{-}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes L^{-}(v)\bigr)=\bigl(\bm{1}\otimes L^{-}(v)\bigr)\,\bigl(L^{-}(u)\otimes\bm{1}\bigr)\,\left(u-v+P\right)

The derivation in the case of
L^{+}
is very similar.

B.1.2
Reduction to the DST
M
-operators

The
M
-operator (
1.25
) and the

L^{+}
-operator (
B.1.1
) are equivalent modulo changing the space of functions, and below we construct
the explicit map between them

\displaystyle L^{+}(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+\partial_{z}z&-\partial_{z}\\
-z&1\end{array}\right)\longrightarrow M(u)=\begin{pmatrix}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}&e^{-x}\\[4.0pt]
-e^{x}\partial_{x}&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}\,.

The first step is the Fourier transformation

\displaystyle\left[F\Psi\right](p)=\hat{\Psi}(p)=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dz\,e^{-\mathrm{i}pz}\,\Psi(z).

(B.38)

In the context of the XXX spin chains we consider the function
\Psi(z)
to be analytic in the upper half-plane
\Im(z)>0
, see
[
ABDK
]
.
For real
z
it is defined as a limit

\Psi(z)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Psi(z+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon)
.
Due to analyticity of the function
\Psi(z)
in upper half-plane
\Im(z)>0

the function
\hat{\Psi}(p)
vanishes for
p<0
and
the formula for the inverse transformation has the form

\displaystyle\left[F^{-1}\hat{\Psi}\right](z)=\Psi(z)=\int\limits_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\,e^{\mathrm{i}pz}\,\hat{\Psi}(p).

(B.39)

Note that
\hat{\Psi}(p)
vanishes for
p=0
as well.
For the point
p=0
we have

\hat{\Psi}(0)=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dz\,\Psi(z)

The Fourier transformation is defined for the functions from

L_{1}(\mathbb{R})
so that this integral is absolutely convergent.
Then
|\Psi(z)|=o(|z|^{-1})
for
|z|\to\infty
, and it is possible to
close the contour of integration in the upper half-plane
\Im(z)>0
, where the integral vanishes due to analyticity of
\Psi(z)
in the upper half-plane.
We have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\,\Psi(z)=\int\limits_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\,e^{\mathrm{i}pz}\,\mathrm{i}p\,\hat{\Psi}(p)\,,

\displaystyle z\,\Psi(z)=\int\limits_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\,\hat{\Psi}(p)\,(-\mathrm{i})\partial_{p}e^{\mathrm{i}pz}=-\mathrm{i}\left.e^{\mathrm{i}pz}\,\hat{\Psi}(p)\right|_{0}^{+\infty}+\int\limits_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\,e^{\mathrm{i}pz}\,(\mathrm{i}\partial_{p})\,\hat{\Psi}(p).

The additional contributions vanish:
for
p\to+\infty
we have
\hat{\Psi}(p)\to 0
due
to Riemann-Lebesgue lemma for integral (
B.38
) with
initial function
\Psi(z)
from
L_{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
for the point
p=0
we have
\hat{\Psi}(0)=0
.
In a more formal way these formulae can be represented
in the following operator form

\displaystyle FzF^{-1}=\mathrm{i}\partial_{z}\ \ \ ;\ \ \ F\partial_{z}F^{-1}=\mathrm{i}z.

(B.40)

The needed transformation of
L^{+}
-operator is

\displaystyle L^{+}(u)\,\Psi(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+\partial_{z}z&-\partial_{z}\\
-z&1\end{array}\right)\,\Psi(z)=\int\limits_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\,e^{\mathrm{i}pz}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u-p\partial_{p}&-\mathrm{i}p\\
-\mathrm{i}\partial_{p}&1\end{array}\right)\,\hat{\Psi}(p)=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{dx}{2\pi}\,\exp(\mathrm{i}ze^{-x}-x)\,(-\mathrm{i})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}&e^{-x}\\
-e^{x}\partial_{x}&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right)\,\hat{\Psi}(e^{-x})=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{dx}{2\pi}\,e^{\mathrm{i}ze^{-x}-x}\,(-\mathrm{i})M(\mathrm{i}u)\,\hat{\Psi}(e^{-x})

and consists of two steps: the first step is the Fourier transformation and then
change of variables — the variable
p
is positive so
that it is possible to perform the change of variables
p=e^{-x}
.
In more formal way the first step is the Fourier transformation (
B.40
)

\displaystyle F\,L^{+}(u)\,F^{-1}=F\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u+\partial_{z}z&-\partial_{z}\\
-z&1\end{array}\right)\,F^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u-z\partial_{z}&-\mathrm{i}z\\
-\mathrm{i}\partial_{z}&1\end{array}\right)=\hat{L}^{+}(u)

and the second step is the change of variables

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{+}(u)\,\hat{\Psi}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}u-z\partial_{z}&-\mathrm{i}z\\
-\mathrm{i}\partial_{z}&1\end{array}\right)\,\hat{\Psi}(z)\longrightarrow M(\mathrm{i}u)\,\hat{\Psi}(e^{-x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}u+\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}&e^{-x}\\
